P versus NP JAH-USEQLOS-000001 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The P versus NP problem asks whether every decision problem whose proposed positive answer can be checked in polynomial time can also be answered in polynomial time. In symbols, P is the class of languages decidable by a deterministic Turing machine in polynomial time, and NP is the class whose yes-instances have polynomial-size certificates checkable in polynomial time. Clearly P is contained in NP. The open equation is whether the containment is equality or strict, and no proof in either direction is known. The central equation is: P = NP ? (P subset NP; is every polynomial-time verifiable language polynomial-time decidable?) A resolution either way would reshape mathematics, cryptography, optimisation, artificial intelligence and science. If P equalled NP, then proof discovery, protein folding models, scheduling and many design problems would become efficiently solvable, while most public-key cryptography would collapse. If P differs from NP, as most experts believe, then the hardness of NP-complete problems is confirmed as a genuine law of computation, and cryptography and heuristic algorithm design rest on firm ground. Either answer would be one of the deepest results ever proved about what machines can do.

Statement

The P versus NP problem asks whether every decision problem whose proposed positive answer can be checked in polynomial time can also be answered in polynomial time. In symbols, P is the class of languages decidable by a deterministic Turing machine in polynomial time, and NP is the class whose yes-instances have polynomial-size certificates checkable in polynomial time. Clearly P is contained in NP. The open equation is whether the containment is equality or strict, and no proof in either direction is known. The equation is: P = NP ? (P subset NP; is every polynomial-time verifiable language polynomial-time decidable?)

History

Stephen Cook formalised the problem in 1971 in his paper on the complexity of theorem proving, and Leonid Levin independently reached the same ideas in 1973. Earlier hints appear in a 1956 letter from Kurt Godel to John von Neumann asking whether theorem proving could be automated in linear or quadratic time, and in a 1955 letter from John Nash to the NSA about exponential code-breaking time. The Clay Mathematics Institute named it one of seven Millennium Prize Problems in 2000, and it remains the most famous open problem in computer science.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

Thousands of problems are known to be NP-complete, so a polynomial algorithm for any one of them would collapse P and NP, yet none has been found. The Cook-Levin theorem established Boolean satisfiability as NP-complete, and Karp's 1972 paper listed twenty-one NP-complete problems. Time hierarchy theorems separate P from EXPTIME, and space hierarchy results separate L from PSPACE, but every known technique stops short of separating P from NP. Relativisation, natural proofs and algebrization barriers explain why the standard approaches cannot settle the question as stated.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies the structure of NP-complete problems through small exhaustive instances: it enumerates satisfying assignments of tiny Boolean formulas and tracks how the count of solutions grows with instance size, looking for a combinatorial invariant that separates easy from hard families. The attempt then tests whether a proposed polynomial-time heuristic succeeds on structured instances and records exactly where it fails. This is a design study only: the small cases are fully checkable, but no pattern in them is claimed to generalise, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, a language L is in NP when there is a polynomial-time relation R and a polynomial p such that x is in L exactly when some y with |y| <= p(|x|) satisfies R(x, y). The conjecture P != NP asserts that no deterministic polynomial-time machine decides every such language. In metamath-style gloss: for all deterministic Turing machines M and all polynomials q, there exists a language L in NP and an input x such that M fails to decide L within q(|x|) steps. The statement is a universal claim over machines and polynomials, which is why a proof must defeat every possible algorithm at once.

Science Proof

A resolution either way would reshape mathematics, cryptography, optimisation, artificial intelligence and science. If P equalled NP, then proof discovery, protein folding models, scheduling and many design problems would become efficiently solvable, while most public-key cryptography would collapse. If P differs from NP, as most experts believe, then the hardness of NP-complete problems is confirmed as a genuine law of computation, and cryptography and heuristic algorithm design rest on firm ground. Either answer would be one of the deepest results ever proved about what machines can do.

Demo

The demo performs a small verified computation related to this problem: it checks a tiny instance exhaustively, or simulates a small case with a fixed seed, and it prints two SHA-256 digests that must match. The demo computes a verified partial result and states plainly what it does not prove. It does not settle the open problem, and it must not be read as doing so.

Program

The program is a full working program in pure Python. It prints the problem statement, the known bounds, runs the same verification as the demo, and prints a summary. It executes cleanly with exit code zero.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[2, 1, 4], [4, -1, 2], [-1, -2, 4], [-2, 1, -4], [4, 1, 2], [-4, -3, -2]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small exhaustive check (4 variables, brute force).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This check counts satisfying assignments of a tiny formula only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

import itertools

CLAUSES = [[2, 1, 4], [4, -1, 2], [-1, -2, 4], [-2, 1, -4], [4, 1, 2], [-4, -3, -2]]
NVAR = 4

        good = True

                good = False
                break

    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines

NP versus co-NP JAH-USEQLOS-000002 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The NP versus co-NP problem asks whether the class NP equals its complement class co-NP. A language is in co-NP when its no-instances have short certificates that a polynomial-time verifier can check, so the question is whether unsatisfiability, non-primality and similar negative claims are as easy to certify as their positive counterparts. If NP equalled co-NP, then every NP problem would have short proofs of both yes and no answers. No proof of equality or inequality is known, and the question is open. The central equation is: NP = co-NP ? (is every language whose no-instances have short certificates also in NP?) Settling NP versus co-NP would decide whether negative answers can be certified as cheaply as positive ones, with consequences for proof systems, automated theorem proving and cryptography. A proof of equality would give short proofs for every tautology and revolutionise formal verification, since a machine could certify that a design has no flaw as easily as it finds one. A proof of inequality would confirm that some truths are inherently harder to refute than to confirm, and would explain why unsatisfiability proving remains hard in practice. Either way the answer shapes the limits of machine reasoning.

Statement

The NP versus co-NP problem asks whether the class NP equals its complement class co-NP. A language is in co-NP when its no-instances have short certificates that a polynomial-time verifier can check, so the question is whether unsatisfiability, non-primality and similar negative claims are as easy to certify as their positive counterparts. If NP equalled co-NP, then every NP problem would have short proofs of both yes and no answers. No proof of equality or inequality is known, and the question is open. The equation is: NP = co-NP ? (is every language whose no-instances have short certificates also in NP?)

History

The classes NP and co-NP were distinguished in the early 1970s as complexity theory took shape after Cook's and Karp's work on NP-completeness. It was quickly noticed that NP-complete problems have no known co-NP certificates: nobody knows a short proof that a formula is unsatisfiable in general. The discovery that primality is in NP and in co-NP, and later in P through the AKS test of 2002, showed that some problems sit in the intersection, but the general question of whether the two classes coincide has resisted every attack for more than fifty years.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that NP equals co-NP exactly when there is a polynomial-size proof system in which every tautology has a short proof, which connects the problem to proof complexity. If any NP-complete problem belonged to co-NP, the polynomial hierarchy would collapse to its first level, which is considered unlikely. Integer factorisation and graph isomorphism are famous problems in NP intersect co-NP that are not known to be in P or NP-complete. No separation and no equality proof exists, and the problem is strictly weaker than P versus NP: P equal to NP would imply NP equals co-NP, but not conversely.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt attacks the problem through small tautologies: it enumerates tiny propositional formulas, computes the shortest resolution refutations of the unsatisfiable ones by exhaustive search, and measures how refutation length grows with formula size. It then tests whether a proposed short-proof construction can be extended from the small cases, recording each failure precisely. This is a design study only: exhaustive data on formulas with a handful of variables cannot establish any asymptotic claim, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, L is in co-NP when its complement is in NP, that is, when there is a polynomial-time relation R and polynomial p such that x is not in L exactly when some y with |y| <= p(|x|) satisfies R(x, y). The conjecture NP != co-NP asserts the existence of a language in NP whose complement has no such short-certificate system. In metamath-style gloss: there exists L in NP such that for every polynomial-time verifier V and every polynomial q, some no-instance x of L has no certificate y of length at most q(|x|) accepted by V. The statement quantifies over all verifiers.

Science Proof

Settling NP versus co-NP would decide whether negative answers can be certified as cheaply as positive ones, with consequences for proof systems, automated theorem proving and cryptography. A proof of equality would give short proofs for every tautology and revolutionise formal verification, since a machine could certify that a design has no flaw as easily as it finds one. A proof of inequality would confirm that some truths are inherently harder to refute than to confirm, and would explain why unsatisfiability proving remains hard in practice. Either way the answer shapes the limits of machine reasoning.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[-4, 1, 3], [-3, 1, -2], [-2, 3, 4], [-4, -3, -1], [3, 2, 4], [-1, 2, -4]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small exhaustive check (4 variables, brute force).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This check counts satisfying assignments of a tiny formula only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

BPP versus P JAH-USEQLOS-000003 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The BPP versus P problem asks whether randomness helps polynomial-time computation. BPP is the class of decision problems solvable by a randomised polynomial-time algorithm that is correct with probability at least two thirds on every input. The question is whether every such problem also has a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm, that is, whether BPP equals P. Most researchers suspect the answer is yes, but no proof exists, and the problem is one of the central open equations of complexity theory. The central equation is: BPP = P ? (can every efficient randomised algorithm be derandomised to a deterministic polynomial-time one?) If BPP equals P, then randomness is provably useless for efficient computation, and every randomised algorithm used in practice could in principle be replaced by a deterministic one with only polynomial slowdown. That would unify the theory of efficient computation and simplify algorithm design, since programmers would never need to trust a coin flip. If instead BPP strictly exceeds P, then randomness is a genuine computational resource, and probabilistic methods in cryptography, sampling and simulation rest on an irreducible foundation. The answer also decides whether pseudorandom generators can be built from hardness alone.

Statement

The BPP versus P problem asks whether randomness helps polynomial-time computation. BPP is the class of decision problems solvable by a randomised polynomial-time algorithm that is correct with probability at least two thirds on every input. The question is whether every such problem also has a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm, that is, whether BPP equals P. Most researchers suspect the answer is yes, but no proof exists, and the problem is one of the central open equations of complexity theory. The equation is: BPP = P ? (can every efficient randomised algorithm be derandomised to a deterministic polynomial-time one?)

History

Randomised algorithms entered complexity theory in the 1970s with the Solovay-Strassen primality test and Rabin's work, and the class BPP was defined in the late 1970s by Gill. The question of derandomisation gained force in the 1990s when Nisan and Wigderson connected it to circuit lower bounds: they showed that strong enough lower bounds against small circuits would imply that BPP equals P. Impagliazzo and Wigderson later proved that either BPP equals P or exponential-time problems require large circuits. The problem has remained open through all of this progress.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that BPP is contained in the polynomial hierarchy, in fact in the second level, and that BPP is contained in P/poly. The Nisan-Wigderson generator shows that if there is a problem in exponential time that needs exponential-size circuits, then BPP equals P, so derandomisation follows from a strong enough hardness assumption. Conversely, Kabanets and Cai showed that derandomisation implies circuit lower bounds. Primality testing, once the flagship randomised algorithm, was derandomised by AKS in 2002, but no general derandomisation theorem is known.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies derandomisation on tiny instances: it takes small randomised decision procedures, enumerates all of their random choices exhaustively, and checks whether a short fixed seed set reproduces the correct answer on every small input. It then measures how the required seed set grows with input size and tests whether a simple pseudorandom generator built from a small hard function fools the procedure. This is a design study only: seed sets for tiny inputs say nothing about asymptotic behaviour, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, L is in BPP when there is a randomised polynomial-time machine M such that for every input x, M accepts x with probability at least two thirds if x is in L, and at most one third otherwise. The conjecture BPP = P asserts that for every such M there is a deterministic polynomial-time machine deciding the same language. In metamath-style gloss: for all randomised polynomial-time machines M there exist a deterministic polynomial-time machine D and a polynomial q such that D decides the language of M within q(|x|) steps on every input. The quantifier over all randomised machines makes the claim strong.

Science Proof

If BPP equals P, then randomness is provably useless for efficient computation, and every randomised algorithm used in practice could in principle be replaced by a deterministic one with only polynomial slowdown. That would unify the theory of efficient computation and simplify algorithm design, since programmers would never need to trust a coin flip. If instead BPP strictly exceeds P, then randomness is a genuine computational resource, and probabilistic methods in cryptography, sampling and simulation rest on an irreducible foundation. The answer also decides whether pseudorandom generators can be built from hardness alone.

Demo

The demo performs a small verified computation related to this problem: it checks a tiny instance exhaustively, or simulates a small case with a fixed seed, and it prints two SHA-256 digests that must match. The demo computes a verified partial result and states plainly what it does not prove. It does not settle the open problem, and it must not be read as doing so.

Exponential Time Hypothesis JAH-USEQLOS-000004 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The Exponential Time Hypothesis, known as ETH, asserts that 3-SAT cannot be decided in subexponential time. More precisely, it states that there is a positive constant c such that no algorithm can decide satisfiability of 3-CNF formulas on n variables in time 2 to the power o(n). The hypothesis is stronger than P different from NP: it does not merely say that no polynomial algorithm exists, it says that no algorithm can do much better than the trivial exponential search. ETH is unproven and is used as a working assumption across complexity theory. The central equation is: 3-SAT needs 2^{Omega(n)} time: is there c > 0 such that no algorithm decides n-variable 3-SAT in 2^{o(n)} time? ETH is the working hypothesis behind fine-grained complexity, the field that explains why problems like edit distance, longest common subsequence and all-pairs shortest paths have resisted faster algorithms. If ETH is true, then the running times of the best known algorithms for dozens of practical problems are essentially optimal, and engineers should optimise constants rather than hunt for breakthroughs. If ETH is false, a subexponential satisfiability algorithm would cascade into faster algorithms across computer science. The hypothesis therefore organises a large part of modern algorithm research.

Statement

The Exponential Time Hypothesis, known as ETH, asserts that 3-SAT cannot be decided in subexponential time. More precisely, it states that there is a positive constant c such that no algorithm can decide satisfiability of 3-CNF formulas on n variables in time 2 to the power o(n). The hypothesis is stronger than P different from NP: it does not merely say that no polynomial algorithm exists, it says that no algorithm can do much better than the trivial exponential search. ETH is unproven and is used as a working assumption across complexity theory. The equation is: 3-SAT needs 2^{Omega(n)} time: is there c > 0 such that no algorithm decides n-variable 3-SAT in 2^{o(n)} time?

History

Impagliazzo and Paturi formulated ETH in 1999, building on the sparsification lemma, which shows that any k-SAT instance can be reduced to a collection of sparse instances with only linearly many clauses. Their work grew out of the study of exact exponential algorithms for satisfiability in the 1990s. Since then ETH has become a standard hardness assumption: hundreds of results in algorithms, parameterised complexity and fine-grained complexity are proved conditional on ETH, showing that many problems cannot be solved substantially faster than their known algorithms unless ETH fails.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

The best known algorithms for 3-SAT run in time roughly 2 to the power 0.39 n, and no algorithm is known to beat the exponential barrier in the exponent. The sparsification lemma is proved, so ETH for 3-SAT is equivalent to ETH for general SAT. It is known that if ETH fails, then many problems in P have surprisingly fast algorithms, and the Strong Exponential Time Hypothesis, which pins the exact base of the exponent for k-SAT, is an even stronger open claim. No proof and no refutation of ETH exists, and its truth is considered plausible but far from certain.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt tests ETH on tiny formulas: it enumerates all 3-CNF formulas on a few variables, runs exhaustive search with a simple branching rule, and records the exact number of search steps as the variable count grows. It then fits the growth curve and checks whether any simple branching heuristic beats the fitted exponential on the small cases. This is a design study only: growth on four or five variables cannot distinguish polynomial from exponential behaviour, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, ETH asserts the existence of a constant c > 0 such that every algorithm deciding 3-SAT needs time at least 2^{c n} on infinitely many n-variable instances, or in the usual formulation, no algorithm runs in 2^{o(n)} time. In metamath-style gloss: there is no algorithm A and no function f(n) = o(n) such that A decides 3-SAT on all n-variable formulas within 2^{f(n)} steps. The statement is a lower bound over all algorithms, which is why it is hard: refuting it needs only one fast algorithm, but proving it needs a reason that defeats every possible algorithm.

Science Proof

ETH is the working hypothesis behind fine-grained complexity, the field that explains why problems like edit distance, longest common subsequence and all-pairs shortest paths have resisted faster algorithms. If ETH is true, then the running times of the best known algorithms for dozens of practical problems are essentially optimal, and engineers should optimise constants rather than hunt for breakthroughs. If ETH is false, a subexponential satisfiability algorithm would cascade into faster algorithms across computer science. The hypothesis therefore organises a large part of modern algorithm research.

Formal equation, in plain words

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the problem be stated in the language of logic, model theory, set theory and computational complexity. Proof sketch

The best known algorithms for 3-SAT run in time roughly 2 to the power 0.39 n, and no algorithm is known to beat the exponential barrier in the exponent. The sparsification lemma is proved, so ETH for 3-SAT is equivalent to ETH for general SAT. It is known that if ETH fails, then many problems in P have surprisingly fast algorithms, and the Strong Exponential Time Hypothesis, which pins the exact base of the exponent for k-SAT, is an even stronger open claim. No proof and no refutation of ETH exists, and its truth is considered plausible but far from certain. The best known partial result is proved by the standard methods of the field, and its proof is a genuine achievement, but its limits are explicit: it covers special cases, or gives bounds rather than exact values, or works under assumptions that the full problem does not grant. The sketch above records what is proved and where it stops, so that no reader mistakes a partial result for a complete answer.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[2, 4, -3], [-1, -3, -2], [1, 2, 4], [1, 2, 3], [-3, -4, -1], [-4, -3, -2]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small exhaustive check (4 variables, brute force).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This check counts satisfying assignments of a tiny formula only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Unique Games Conjecture JAH-USEQLOS-000005 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The Unique Games Conjecture, proposed by Subhash Khot in 2002, asserts that it is NP-hard to tell apart unique games that are almost satisfiable from ones that are almost entirely unsatisfiable. A unique game is a constraint satisfaction problem on a graph where each edge constraint is a permutation: fixing the label of one endpoint determines the label of the other. The conjecture says that for every small epsilon and delta there is a label-set size k making the gap problem NP-hard. The conjecture is open; a claimed proof reported in 2026 has not been independently verified, so the record treats the conjecture as open. The central equation is: For all small eps, delta > 0 there is k such that (1-delta, eps)-gap Unique Label Cover is NP-hard. If the Unique Games Conjecture is true, then the best known approximation algorithms for a host of optimisation problems are provably optimal, and the theory of approximation has a clean, complete picture: semidefinite programming achieves everything that efficient computation can. If it is false, then better approximation algorithms exist for problems like Max-Cut and Vertex Cover, and a new algorithmic technique awaits discovery. Either answer would close one of the largest open chapters in theoretical computer science and redirect research in optimisation, machine learning and operations research.

Statement

The Unique Games Conjecture, proposed by Subhash Khot in 2002, asserts that it is NP-hard to tell apart unique games that are almost satisfiable from ones that are almost entirely unsatisfiable. A unique game is a constraint satisfaction problem on a graph where each edge constraint is a permutation: fixing the label of one endpoint determines the label of the other. The conjecture says that for every small epsilon and delta there is a label-set size k making the gap problem NP-hard. The conjecture is open; a claimed proof reported in 2026 has not been independently verified, so the record treats the conjecture as open. The equation is: For all small eps, delta > 0 there is k such that (1-delta, eps)-gap Unique Label Cover is NP-hard.

History

Khot proposed the conjecture in 2002 to explain why many approximation problems resist better algorithms. It quickly became central: assuming the conjecture, the Goemans-Williamson approximation for Max-Cut and many other approximation ratios are exactly optimal. In 2008 Khot, Kindler, Mossel and O'Donnell proved that the conjecture implies the optimal inapproximability of Max-Cut. Work on the related 2-to-2 Games Conjecture, a weaker statement, advanced in 2018, but the full Unique Games Conjecture itself remains unproven, and expert opinion is roughly evenly divided on whether it is true.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the Unique Games Conjecture implies optimal hardness of approximation for Max-Cut, Vertex Cover, Max-2-SAT and many other problems, and that semidefinite programming algorithms match those bounds, so the conjecture sits exactly at the frontier of approximation theory. The 2-to-2 Games Conjecture was proved in 2018 by Dinur, Khot, Kindler, Minzer and Safra, which settled a weaker variant. Subexponential-time algorithms exist for unique games, and the conjecture is known to be false for some restricted families. No proof and no refutation of the full conjecture is accepted, and the 2026 claimed proof awaits independent verification.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies unique games on tiny graphs: it enumerates all labellings of small unique games, computes their exact values by exhaustive search, and compares the true value with the value returned by a simple semidefinite relaxation on the same instances. It then records the gap between the two and tests whether any small instance violates the pattern predicted by the conjecture. This is a design study only: exhaustive data on graphs with a handful of vertices cannot establish hardness for large instances, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, an instance is a graph with a permutation constraint on each edge, and its value is the largest fraction of constraints any labelling satisfies. The conjecture asserts that for all sufficiently small eps, delta > 0 there exists k such that distinguishing value at least 1 - delta from value at most eps is NP-hard. In metamath-style gloss: for all eps, delta in (0, 1) small, there exists a natural number k such that the (1 - delta, eps)-gap label cover problem with k labels is NP-hard. The statement quantifies over all gap parameters and all label sizes, which is why partial results on fixed k do not settle it.

Science Proof

If the Unique Games Conjecture is true, then the best known approximation algorithms for a host of optimisation problems are provably optimal, and the theory of approximation has a clean, complete picture: semidefinite programming achieves everything that efficient computation can. If it is false, then better approximation algorithms exist for problems like Max-Cut and Vertex Cover, and a new algorithmic technique awaits discovery. Either answer would close one of the largest open chapters in theoretical computer science and redirect research in optimisation, machine learning and operations research.

Polynomial Hierarchy Non-Collapse JAH-USEQLOS-000006 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The polynomial hierarchy is a tower of complexity classes built by alternating existential and universal quantifiers over polynomial-time predicates. Its k-th level adds k alternating quantifiers, and the hierarchy is said to collapse if some level equals all higher levels. The open problem is whether the hierarchy is strict: whether each level is genuinely more powerful than the one below, so that no finite level captures the whole hierarchy. No separation between any two adjacent levels is known, and the strictness of the hierarchy is a central open equation of complexity theory. The central equation is: PH does not collapse: for every k, Sigma_k^p is strictly contained in Sigma_{k+1}^p. The strictness of the polynomial hierarchy would confirm that alternating quantifiers genuinely add computational power, and would organise thousands of problems into a fine-grained ladder of difficulty. It underlies the belief that problems like circuit minimisation and certain quantified Boolean formulas are strictly harder than NP-complete problems. If the hierarchy collapsed, then much of complexity theory would simplify drastically, and many hardness assumptions used in cryptography would need rethinking. The question therefore measures how much structure the world of efficient computation really has.

Statement

The polynomial hierarchy is a tower of complexity classes built by alternating existential and universal quantifiers over polynomial-time predicates. Its k-th level adds k alternating quantifiers, and the hierarchy is said to collapse if some level equals all higher levels. The open problem is whether the hierarchy is strict: whether each level is genuinely more powerful than the one below, so that no finite level captures the whole hierarchy. No separation between any two adjacent levels is known, and the strictness of the hierarchy is a central open equation of complexity theory. The equation is: PH does not collapse: for every k, Sigma_k^p is strictly contained in Sigma_{k+1}^p.

History

Meyer and Stockmeyer introduced the polynomial hierarchy in 1972, generalising the classes NP and co-NP into an infinite tower. Stockmeyer showed that the hierarchy is contained in PSPACE, and that if P equals NP then the whole hierarchy collapses to P. Karp and Lipton showed that if NP were contained in P/poly, the hierarchy would collapse to its second level, which is considered strong evidence that NP is not in P/poly. Despite fifty years of work, no unconditional separation of any two levels has been proved, and the hierarchy remains a conjectural tower.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that if the hierarchy collapsed at any level, then the entire tower above that level would be equal to it, so a single collapse would flatten everything. The classes Sigma_2^p and Pi_2^p contain natural complete problems such as minimising Boolean circuits and certain game problems. Toda's theorem shows that the whole polynomial hierarchy is contained in P with a counting oracle, and the time hierarchy theorem separates P from EXPTIME, which lies above the hierarchy. But no technique is known that separates even the first level from the second, and relativisation results show that standard diagonalisation cannot do it.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies the hierarchy on tiny quantified formulas: it enumerates small quantified Boolean formulas with one and two quantifier alternations, decides them by exhaustive search, and measures the growth of the search cost as the number of variables increases. It then tests whether a proposed algorithm for two-level formulas can be simulated by a one-level procedure on the small cases, recording where the simulation breaks. This is a design study only: cost growth on a few variables proves nothing about the asymptotic separation, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, Sigma_0^p is P, Sigma_{k+1}^p is the class of languages L such that x is in L exactly when there exist y_1, for all y_2, and so on through k+1 alternating quantifiers, with each y_i polynomially bounded and the final predicate polynomial-time. The conjecture is that Sigma_k^p is strictly contained in Sigma_{k+1}^p for every k. In metamath-style gloss: for all k, there exists a language L in Sigma_{k+1}^p such that for every Sigma_k^p machine and every polynomial q, the machine fails to decide L within q(|x|) steps on some input. The statement is an infinite family of separations.

Science Proof

The strictness of the polynomial hierarchy would confirm that alternating quantifiers genuinely add computational power, and would organise thousands of problems into a fine-grained ladder of difficulty. It underlies the belief that problems like circuit minimisation and certain quantified Boolean formulas are strictly harder than NP-complete problems. If the hierarchy collapsed, then much of complexity theory would simplify drastically, and many hardness assumptions used in cryptography would need rethinking. The question therefore measures how much structure the world of efficient computation really has.

History

Meyer and Stockmeyer introduced the polynomial hierarchy in 1972, generalising the classes NP and co-NP into an infinite tower. Stockmeyer showed that the hierarchy is contained in PSPACE, and that if P equals NP then the whole hierarchy collapses to P. Karp and Lipton showed that if NP were contained in P/poly, the hierarchy would collapse to its second level, which is considered strong evidence that NP is not in P/poly. Despite fifty years of work, no unconditional separation of any two levels has been proved, and the hierarchy remains a conjectural tower. This history shows how the problem grew from a specific question into a central open problem of logic, model theory, set theory and computational complexity, and why each generation of researchers found it harder than the last.

Problem

The polynomial hierarchy is a tower of complexity classes built by alternating existential and universal quantifiers over polynomial-time predicates. Its k-th level adds k alternating quantifiers, and the hierarchy is said to collapse if some level equals all higher levels. The open problem is whether the hierarchy is strict: whether each level is genuinely more powerful than the one below, so that no finite level captures the whole hierarchy. No separation between any two adjacent levels is known, and the strictness of the hierarchy is a central open equation of complexity theory. In symbols, the problem is: PH does not collapse: for every k, Sigma_k^p is strictly contained in Sigma_{k+1}^p. A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

The Signature attempt studies the hierarchy on tiny quantified formulas: it enumerates small quantified Boolean formulas with one and two quantifier alternations, decides them by exhaustive search, and measures the growth of the search cost as the number of variables increases. It then tests whether a proposed algorithm for two-level formulas can be simulated by a one-level procedure on the small cases, recording where the simulation breaks. This is a design study only: cost growth on a few variables proves nothing about the asymptotic separation, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED. The attempt is a design study: it works only on tiny, fully checkable instances, and every result it produces is labelled with its verification status. Nothing in the attempt is presented as a proof, and the attempt does not claim to settle the open problem.

Proof sketch

It is known that if the hierarchy collapsed at any level, then the entire tower above that level would be equal to it, so a single collapse would flatten everything. The classes Sigma_2^p and Pi_2^p contain natural complete problems such as minimising Boolean circuits and certain game problems. Toda's theorem shows that the whole polynomial hierarchy is contained in P with a counting oracle, and the time hierarchy theorem separates P from EXPTIME, which lies above the hierarchy. But no technique is known that separates even the first level from the second, and relativisation results show that standard diagonalisation cannot do it. The best known partial result is proved by the standard methods of the field, and its proof is a genuine achievement, but its limits are explicit: it covers special cases, or gives bounds rather than exact values, or works under assumptions that the full problem does not grant. The sketch above records what is proved and where it stops, so that no reader mistakes a partial result for a complete answer.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[-2, -4, -3], [-1, 4, 3], [1, 4, 3], [-2, 4, -3], [3, -2, 1], [-4, 1, -3]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small exhaustive check (4 variables, brute force).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This check counts satisfying assignments of a tiny formula only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

L versus NL JAH-USEQLOS-000007 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The L versus NL problem asks whether nondeterministic logarithmic space is more powerful than deterministic logarithmic space. L is the class of decision problems solvable by a deterministic Turing machine using only logarithmically much memory, and NL allows the machine to make nondeterministic guesses while using the same small amount of memory. The question is whether every NL problem has a deterministic log-space algorithm. Reachability in directed graphs is the canonical NL-complete problem, and no deterministic log-space algorithm for it is known. The central equation is: L = NL ? (is every problem solvable in nondeterministic logarithmic space also solvable deterministically?) Settling L versus NL would complete the map of small-space computation and decide whether nondeterminism buys anything when memory is scarce. A proof of equality would give efficient deterministic algorithms for directed reachability and every problem that reduces to it, with applications in verification, model checking and database query evaluation, where log-space algorithms are the practical standard. A proof of inequality would confirm that guessing is a genuine resource even in tiny memory, and would explain why directed reachability has resisted deterministic log-space algorithms for decades. Either answer sharpens the theory of feasible computation.

Statement

The L versus NL problem asks whether nondeterministic logarithmic space is more powerful than deterministic logarithmic space. L is the class of decision problems solvable by a deterministic Turing machine using only logarithmically much memory, and NL allows the machine to make nondeterministic guesses while using the same small amount of memory. The question is whether every NL problem has a deterministic log-space algorithm. Reachability in directed graphs is the canonical NL-complete problem, and no deterministic log-space algorithm for it is known. The equation is: L = NL ? (is every problem solvable in nondeterministic logarithmic space also solvable deterministically?)

History

Logarithmic-space classes were defined in the 1960s and 1970s as researchers mapped the low end of the complexity landscape. Savitch proved in 1970 that nondeterministic space s(n) can be simulated deterministically in space s(n) squared, which for logarithmic space gives NL contained in SPACE(log squared n), but not in L. Immerman and Szelepcsenyi proved in 1987 that NL is closed under complement, a surprising result that won the Goedel Prize, yet the equality of L and NL remains open. Reingold's 2005 proof that undirected reachability is in L showed that the directed case is the true frontier.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that L is contained in NL, which is contained in P, and that NL equals co-NL by the Immerman-Szelepcsenyi theorem. Undirected graph reachability is in L by Reingold's algorithm using expander graphs and universal traversal sequences. Directed reachability is NL-complete, so L equals NL exactly when directed reachability is in L. The best known deterministic space bound for directed reachability is roughly log squared n divided by log log n, from Savitch's algorithm improved by later work, but no logarithmic-space algorithm is known and no proof of impossibility exists.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies reachability on tiny directed graphs: it enumerates all small directed graphs, computes reachability by exhaustive search, and measures the memory used by a simple deterministic traversal against the size of the graph. It then tests whether a proposed log-space traversal rule succeeds on every small graph, recording each graph where it fails. This is a design study only: behaviour on graphs with a handful of vertices says nothing about asymptotic space bounds, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, L is the class of languages decidable by a deterministic Turing machine using O(log n) work-tape cells, and NL allows a nondeterministic machine with the same space bound. The conjecture L != NL asserts the existence of a language in NL that no deterministic log-space machine decides. In metamath-style gloss: there exists L in NL such that for every deterministic Turing machine M and every constant c, some input x has M using more than c log |x| cells or giving the wrong answer on L. The statement quantifies over all deterministic machines and all space constants, which is why it resists proof.

Science Proof

Settling L versus NL would complete the map of small-space computation and decide whether nondeterminism buys anything when memory is scarce. A proof of equality would give efficient deterministic algorithms for directed reachability and every problem that reduces to it, with applications in verification, model checking and database query evaluation, where log-space algorithms are the practical standard. A proof of inequality would confirm that guessing is a genuine resource even in tiny memory, and would explain why directed reachability has resisted deterministic log-space algorithms for decades. Either answer sharpens the theory of feasible computation.

PSPACE versus EXPTIME JAH-USEQLOS-000008 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The PSPACE versus EXPTIME problem asks whether polynomial space equals exponential time. PSPACE is the class of problems solvable with a polynomial amount of memory, and EXPTIME is the class solvable in exponential time. It is known that PSPACE is contained in EXPTIME, since a polynomial-space machine has only exponentially many configurations and must either halt or repeat one. The open equation is whether the containment is strict: whether there is a problem in EXPTIME that genuinely needs more than polynomial space. The central equation is: PSPACE = EXPTIME ? (is every problem solvable in polynomial space also solvable in exponential time, and vice versa?) A separation would confirm that time and space are genuinely different resources at the exponential scale, and would complete a central piece of the complexity map. It would also locate the strict step in the chain from P to EXPTIME, which would focus research on the right separation problem. Equality, by contrast, would be a shock: it would mean that every exponential-time computation can be reorganised to use only polynomial memory, collapsing a distinction that theorists have treated as fundamental. Either answer would be a landmark in understanding what computers can do with limited resources.

Statement

The PSPACE versus EXPTIME problem asks whether polynomial space equals exponential time. PSPACE is the class of problems solvable with a polynomial amount of memory, and EXPTIME is the class solvable in exponential time. It is known that PSPACE is contained in EXPTIME, since a polynomial-space machine has only exponentially many configurations and must either halt or repeat one. The open equation is whether the containment is strict: whether there is a problem in EXPTIME that genuinely needs more than polynomial space. The equation is: PSPACE = EXPTIME ? (is every problem solvable in polynomial space also solvable in exponential time, and vice versa?)

History

The classes PSPACE and EXPTIME were defined in the early 1970s as complexity theory organised itself into space and time classes. The time hierarchy theorem separated P from EXPTIME, and the space hierarchy theorem separated L from PSPACE, but the relationship between PSPACE and EXPTIME has never been settled. The problem is one of the classic separations listed in every complexity textbook: P is contained in NP, which is contained in PSPACE, which is contained in EXPTIME, and it is known that at least one of the containments in this chain is strict, but nobody knows which one.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that P is strictly contained in EXPTIME by the deterministic time hierarchy theorem, and that L is strictly contained in PSPACE by the space hierarchy theorem. Quantified Boolean formulas are PSPACE-complete, and many natural games, such as generalised chess and Go on an n by n board, are EXPTIME-complete. The containment PSPACE in EXPTIME is proved by simulating a space-bounded machine and counting configurations. No separation is known, and the problem is entwined with P versus NP and NP versus PSPACE: settling any one of those would narrow down where the strict containment in the chain must lie.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies the question on tiny machines: it enumerates small Turing machines with a few states, runs them on short inputs while counting time steps and tape cells used, and records the trade-off between time and space for each machine. It then tests whether any small machine exhibits the exponential-time, super-polynomial-space behaviour the separation would require. This is a design study only: a handful of tiny machines cannot represent the asymptotic classes, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, PSPACE is the class of languages decidable by a deterministic Turing machine using polynomial space, and EXPTIME is the class decidable in time 2^{p(n)} for some polynomial p. The conjecture PSPACE != EXPTIME asserts a language in EXPTIME outside PSPACE. In metamath-style gloss: there exists L in EXPTIME such that for every deterministic Turing machine M and every polynomial q, M either uses more than q(|x|) space on some input or fails to decide L. The statement is a universal claim over all machines and polynomials, which is why a diagonal argument that works for time alone does not settle it.

Science Proof

A separation would confirm that time and space are genuinely different resources at the exponential scale, and would complete a central piece of the complexity map. It would also locate the strict step in the chain from P to EXPTIME, which would focus research on the right separation problem. Equality, by contrast, would be a shock: it would mean that every exponential-time computation can be reorganised to use only polynomial memory, collapsing a distinction that theorists have treated as fundamental. Either answer would be a landmark in understanding what computers can do with limited resources.

The demo performs a small verified computation related to this problem: it checks a tiny instance exhaustively, or simulates a small case with a fixed seed, and it prints two SHA-256 digests that must match. The demo computes a verified partial result and states plainly what it does not prove. It does not settle the open problem, and it must not be read as doing so.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[-1, 4, -2], [-4, 2, 3], [2, 4, -1], [4, -2, 1], [-2, -1, 4], [-2, -3, 4]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small exhaustive check (4 variables, brute force).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This check counts satisfying assignments of a tiny formula only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Circuit Lower Bounds for Explicit Functions JAH-USEQLOS-000009 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The circuit lower bound problem asks whether some explicit problem in NP requires Boolean circuits of superpolynomial size. A Boolean circuit is a network of AND, OR and NOT gates, and P/poly is the class of languages decidable by polynomial-size circuit families. The question is whether NP is contained in P/poly, or whether some explicit NP language, such as satisfiability, needs circuits that grow faster than any polynomial. No superpolynomial lower bound for any explicit NP problem is known, and the problem is one of the deepest open equations in complexity theory. The central equation is: NP not subset of P/poly: does some explicit NP language need superpolynomial-size circuits? A superpolynomial circuit lower bound for an explicit NP problem would separate NP from P/poly and, through Karp-Lipton, would confirm that the polynomial hierarchy does not collapse. It would also vindicate the belief that some computational problems are hard in a very concrete, physical sense: no hardware, however cleverly wired, could solve them quickly. Conversely, if NP were contained in P/poly, then non-uniform hardware could in principle crack NP-complete problems, with dramatic implications for cryptography and chip design. The problem is therefore both a mathematical and a practical frontier.

Statement

The circuit lower bound problem asks whether some explicit problem in NP requires Boolean circuits of superpolynomial size. A Boolean circuit is a network of AND, OR and NOT gates, and P/poly is the class of languages decidable by polynomial-size circuit families. The question is whether NP is contained in P/poly, or whether some explicit NP language, such as satisfiability, needs circuits that grow faster than any polynomial. No superpolynomial lower bound for any explicit NP problem is known, and the problem is one of the deepest open equations in complexity theory. The equation is: NP not subset of P/poly: does some explicit NP language need superpolynomial-size circuits?

History

Shannon proved in 1949 that most Boolean functions need exponential-size circuits, but his argument was by counting and gave no explicit hard function. Research since then has chased an explicit example: Razborov proved superpolynomial lower bounds for monotone circuits in 1985, and Razborov and Rudich proved in 1997 that the natural proofs barrier blocks a whole family of approaches. Williams proved in 2011 that NEXPTIME is not contained in ACC0, the first superpolynomial lower bound for a uniform class against a restricted circuit class. The unrestricted case for NP remains open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that if NP were contained in P/poly, the polynomial hierarchy would collapse to its second level by the Karp-Lipton theorem, which is considered unlikely. Monotone circuit lower bounds are proved for clique and matching, and constant-depth lower bounds are proved for parity by Furst, Saxe and Sipser and by Ajtai. Williams' ACC0 lower bound for NEXPTIME is the strongest known result against a natural circuit class. But for general circuits with no restriction, the best known lower bound for an explicit function is only about 3n, far from the superpolynomial bound the problem asks for.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies tiny circuits: it enumerates all Boolean circuits with a few gates on three inputs, computes exactly which functions each circuit decides, and records the smallest circuit size for a small NP-flavoured function such as 3-clique on a tiny graph. It then tests whether a proposed circuit construction can be extended one gate at a time, recording where the extension fails. This is a design study only: minimum sizes for three-input functions say nothing about asymptotic circuit complexity, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, a language L is in P/poly when there is a polynomial p and a family of circuits C_n, one for each input length n, each of size at most p(n), such that C_n decides L on inputs of length n. The conjecture NP not subset of P/poly asserts a language in NP that no such family decides. In metamath-style gloss: there exists L in NP such that for every polynomial p and every circuit family of size at most p(n), some input x has the circuit giving the wrong answer for L. The statement quantifies over all circuit families, which is why counting arguments that work for most functions cannot name the explicit hard language.

Science Proof

A superpolynomial circuit lower bound for an explicit NP problem would separate NP from P/poly and, through Karp-Lipton, would confirm that the polynomial hierarchy does not collapse. It would also vindicate the belief that some computational problems are hard in a very concrete, physical sense: no hardware, however cleverly wired, could solve them quickly. Conversely, if NP were contained in P/poly, then non-uniform hardware could in principle crack NP-complete problems, with dramatic implications for cryptography and chip design. The problem is therefore both a mathematical and a practical frontier.

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[-1, 2, -4], [2, 4, -1], [4, -1, -2], [1, -4, 3], [3, -2, -4], [-2, -3, 1]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small exhaustive check (4 variables, brute force).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)

    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()

Full working program

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[-1, 2, -4], [2, 4, -1], [4, -1, -2], [1, -4, 3], [3, -2, -4], [-2, -3, 1]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines

Monotone circuit lower bounds are proved for clique and matching, and constant-depth lower bounds are proved for parity by Furst, Saxe and Sipser and by Ajtai. Williams' ACC0 lower bound for NEXPTIME is the strongest known result aga")

TC0 versus NC1 JAH-USEQLOS-000010 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The TC0 versus NC1 problem asks whether constant-depth threshold circuits are as powerful as logarithmic-depth Boolean circuits. TC0 is the class of languages decidable by uniform families of constant-depth circuits with AND, OR, NOT and majority gates, while NC1 uses logarithmic-depth circuits with bounded fan-in AND and OR gates. It is known that TC0 is contained in NC1, since a majority gate can be simulated by a logarithmic-depth circuit. The open equation is whether the containment is strict: whether some NC1 language needs more than constant depth with threshold gates. The central equation is: TC0 = NC1 ? (can threshold circuits of constant depth simulate logarithmic-depth Boolean circuits?) Separating TC0 from NC1 would confirm that depth is a genuine resource even when threshold gates are allowed, and would clarify the power of the neural-style computation that threshold circuits model. It would also separate two natural models of parallel computation and explain why some fast parallel algorithms seem to need logarithmic depth. Equality would be equally striking: it would mean that every logarithmic-depth computation can be flattened to constant depth with majority gates, giving a powerful simulation result for hardware design. Either answer advances the theory of parallel computation.

Statement

The TC0 versus NC1 problem asks whether constant-depth threshold circuits are as powerful as logarithmic-depth Boolean circuits. TC0 is the class of languages decidable by uniform families of constant-depth circuits with AND, OR, NOT and majority gates, while NC1 uses logarithmic-depth circuits with bounded fan-in AND and OR gates. It is known that TC0 is contained in NC1, since a majority gate can be simulated by a logarithmic-depth circuit. The open equation is whether the containment is strict: whether some NC1 language needs more than constant depth with threshold gates. The equation is: TC0 = NC1 ? (can threshold circuits of constant depth simulate logarithmic-depth Boolean circuits?)

History

The classes NC and TC were defined in the 1980s as researchers studied parallel computation and the power of shallow circuits. Cook showed in 1985 that NC1 captures exactly the languages recognisable by polynomial-size Boolean formulas, and the connection between threshold circuits and neural computation made TC0 a natural object. Lower bounds for constant-depth circuits without threshold gates were proved in the 1980s, but with majority gates the problem has resisted all methods. The separation of TC0 from NC1 is now a standard benchmark problem for circuit complexity.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that TC0 is contained in NC1, and that NC1 is contained in L, which is contained in NL, which is contained in P. The parity function is in TC0 but not in AC0, showing that threshold gates add real power at constant depth. The integer division and iterated multiplication functions are in TC0, while it is not known whether they are in NC1 with smaller depth. No separation between TC0 and NC1 is known, and the problem is closely related to whether the Boolean formula value problem and similar NC1-complete problems can be solved by constant-depth threshold circuits.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies tiny threshold circuits: it enumerates small constant-depth threshold circuits on a few inputs, computes exactly which functions they decide, and compares them with the functions decided by small logarithmic-depth Boolean circuits on the same inputs. It records the smallest threshold depth that reproduces each small NC1-style function and tests whether a proposed flattening construction works on the small cases. This is a design study only: small-input behaviour cannot establish any asymptotic separation, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, TC0 is the class of languages decidable by uniform constant-depth polynomial-size circuit families with majority gates of unbounded fan-in, and NC1 uses logarithmic-depth polynomial-size families with bounded fan-in. The conjecture TC0 != NC1 asserts a language in NC1 outside TC0. In metamath-style gloss: there exists L in NC1 such that for every constant depth d and every polynomial p, some input length n has no depth-d threshold circuit of size p(n) deciding L on length n. The statement quantifies over all depths and all circuit families, which is why it is hard to prove.

Science Proof

Separating TC0 from NC1 would confirm that depth is a genuine resource even when threshold gates are allowed, and would clarify the power of the neural-style computation that threshold circuits model. It would also separate two natural models of parallel computation and explain why some fast parallel algorithms seem to need logarithmic depth. Equality would be equally striking: it would mean that every logarithmic-depth computation can be flattened to constant depth with majority gates, giving a powerful simulation result for hardware design. Either answer advances the theory of parallel computation.

The demo performs a small verified computation related to this problem: it checks a tiny instance exhaustively, or simulates a small case with a fixed seed, and it prints two SHA-256 digests that must match. The demo computes a verified partial result and states plainly what it does not prove. It does not settle the open problem, and it must not be read as doing so.

The program is a full working program in pure Python. It prints the problem statement, the known bounds, runs the same verification as the demo, and prints a summary. It executes cleanly with exit code zero.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[4, 2, 3], [-1, 2, -3], [-1, -4, 2], [-3, -1, -2], [4, -1, -2], [4, -2, -3]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small exhaustive check (4 variables, brute force).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This check counts satisfying assignments of a tiny formula only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()
