Frege versus Extended Frege JAH-USEQLOS-000011 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The Frege versus Extended Frege problem asks whether the extended Frege proof system is superpolynomially stronger than the Frege system. A Frege system is a standard textbook proof system for propositional logic, with finitely many axiom schemes and rules, and extended Frege adds the ability to introduce new variables as abbreviations for formulas. The question is whether there is a family of tautologies whose shortest extended Frege proofs are superpolynomially shorter than their shortest Frege proofs. No such family is known, and the separation is a central open problem of proof complexity. The central equation is: Does Extended Frege have superpolynomially shorter proofs than Frege for some tautologies? Separating Frege from extended Frege would be the first superpolynomial lower bound for a strong proof system and would show that the ability to name and reuse subformulas is a genuine source of proof power. It would also bring the field closer to separating NP from co-NP, since strong proof systems are the proof-theoretic shadow of that problem. If instead Frege simulates extended Frege, then abbreviation adds nothing essential, and automated theorem provers based on Frege-style systems lose nothing by avoiding extension variables. Either answer would reshape proof complexity and automated reasoning.

Statement

The Frege versus Extended Frege problem asks whether the extended Frege proof system is superpolynomially stronger than the Frege system. A Frege system is a standard textbook proof system for propositional logic, with finitely many axiom schemes and rules, and extended Frege adds the ability to introduce new variables as abbreviations for formulas. The question is whether there is a family of tautologies whose shortest extended Frege proofs are superpolynomially shorter than their shortest Frege proofs. No such family is known, and the separation is a central open problem of proof complexity. The equation is: Does Extended Frege have superpolynomially shorter proofs than Frege for some tautologies?

History

Cook and Reckhow founded proof complexity in 1979 by defining propositional proof systems and connecting their strength to NP versus co-NP: NP equals co-NP exactly when some proof system has polynomial-size proofs for all tautologies. Frege systems were studied as the canonical strong systems, and extended Frege was introduced as a natural strengthening. Lower bounds were proved for weak systems such as resolution and bounded-depth Frege in the 1980s and 1990s, but Frege itself has resisted all lower bound attempts, and the gap between Frege and extended Frege remains open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that extended Frege can simulate Frege with only polynomial overhead, and that extended Frege is equivalent in strength to the circuit-based system of extended Frege, which corresponds to polynomial-size Boolean circuits. Exponential lower bounds are known for resolution, for bounded-depth Frege, and for the cutting planes system on some formulas. But no superpolynomial lower bound for Frege is known for any family of tautologies, and no family is known where extended Frege beats Frege by more than a polynomial factor. The problem is therefore wide open on both sides.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies tiny proofs: it enumerates small propositional tautologies, searches exhaustively for the shortest Frege-style proofs and the shortest extended-Frege-style proofs, and records the ratio of the two lengths as the formula grows. It then tests whether a proposed abbreviation scheme shortens the small proofs and records where it fails to help. This is a design study only: proof lengths for formulas with a handful of variables cannot establish any asymptotic separation, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, a Frege proof is a sequence of formulas, each an axiom or derived by a rule from earlier ones, ending in the tautology to be proved, and its size is the total number of symbols. Extended Frege allows lines that define a new variable as an abbreviation for a formula, which can compress proofs enormously. The conjecture is that some tautology family needs superpolynomially larger Frege proofs than extended Frege proofs. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a family of tautologies T_n such that the shortest Frege proof of T_n has size superpolynomial in n while the shortest extended Frege proof has size polynomial in n.

Science Proof

Separating Frege from extended Frege would be the first superpolynomial lower bound for a strong proof system and would show that the ability to name and reuse subformulas is a genuine source of proof power. It would also bring the field closer to separating NP from co-NP, since strong proof systems are the proof-theoretic shadow of that problem. If instead Frege simulates extended Frege, then abbreviation adds nothing essential, and automated theorem provers based on Frege-style systems lose nothing by avoiding extension variables. Either answer would reshape proof complexity and automated reasoning.

The best known partial result is proved by the standard methods of the field, and its proof is a genuine achievement, but its limits are explicit: it covers special cases, or gives bounds rather than exact values, or works under assumptions that the full problem does not grant. The sketch above records what is proved and where it stops, so that no reader mistakes a partial result for a complete answer.

Results reported

It is known that extended Frege can simulate Frege with only polynomial overhead, and that extended Frege is equivalent in strength to the circuit-based system of extended Frege, which corresponds to polynomial-size Boolean circuits. Exponential lower bounds are known for resolution, for bounded-depth Frege, and for the cutting planes system on some formulas. But no superpolynomial lower bound for Frege is known for any family of tautologies, and no family is known where extended Frege beats Frege by more than a polynomial factor. The problem is therefore wide open on both sides. These are the verified partial results, bounds and computation limits for this problem. Working demo program

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[-4, -1, -2], [1, -2, 3], [2, 3, 1], [3, -1, 4], [2, -4, -1], [-4, -3, -1]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small exhaustive check (4 variables, brute force).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This check counts satisfying assignments of a tiny formula only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Natural Proofs Barrier JAH-USEQLOS-000012 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The natural proofs barrier asks whether the obstacle identified by Razborov and Rudich in 1997 can be circumvented. A natural proof of a circuit lower bound is one that identifies a large, efficiently recognisable property of Boolean functions that no small circuit can compute. Razborov and Rudich showed that, if strong pseudorandom generators exist, then no natural proof can establish a superpolynomial lower bound against general circuits. The open equation is whether some non-natural method can break through: whether a lower bound proof exists that avoids the barrier entirely. The central equation is: Can the natural proofs barrier be circumvented to prove superpolynomial circuit lower bounds? Circumventing the natural proofs barrier would unlock the first superpolynomial circuit lower bound for an explicit problem and would settle the NP versus P/poly question. It would also show that mathematics can prove hardness without contradicting the security of cryptography, resolving a tension that has shaped complexity theory for a generation. If the barrier turns out to be absolute in a stronger sense, then circuit lower bounds may be unprovable with current methods, and the field would need entirely new foundations. Either way, the barrier is the central strategic question of lower bound research.

Statement

The natural proofs barrier asks whether the obstacle identified by Razborov and Rudich in 1997 can be circumvented. A natural proof of a circuit lower bound is one that identifies a large, efficiently recognisable property of Boolean functions that no small circuit can compute. Razborov and Rudich showed that, if strong pseudorandom generators exist, then no natural proof can establish a superpolynomial lower bound against general circuits. The open equation is whether some non-natural method can break through: whether a lower bound proof exists that avoids the barrier entirely. The equation is: Can the natural proofs barrier be circumvented to prove superpolynomial circuit lower bounds?

History

Razborov and Rudich published the natural proofs paper in 1997, explaining why decades of work on circuit lower bounds had stalled: the standard techniques all had the natural property, and therefore could not work if cryptography is secure. The result redirected the field toward algebraic methods, such as the geometric complexity theory programme of Mulmuley and Sohoni, and toward meta-mathematical results about the limits of proof methods. Williams' 2011 lower bound for NEXPTIME against ACC0 used a non-natural argument, showing that the barrier can be sidestepped for restricted classes, but the general case remains open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that natural proofs cannot prove superpolynomial lower bounds against P/poly if strong pseudorandom generators exist, which follows from the existence of one-way functions. The barrier applies to any proof that works by exhibiting a large constructive property of hard functions. Non-natural approaches that have been tried include algebraic geometry, the polynomial method, and Williams' algorithmic method, which turns a faster satisfiability algorithm into a lower bound. None of these has yet produced a superpolynomial lower bound for an explicit NP problem against general circuits, so the barrier still stands in practice.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies the barrier on tiny functions: it enumerates all Boolean functions on three inputs, computes which of them have a small natural property such as high sensitivity or large Fourier mass, and checks whether any small circuit fails exactly on the functions with the property. It then tests whether a proposed non-natural property, based on a global algebraic invariant, distinguishes hard from easy small functions. This is a design study only: properties of three-input functions cannot speak to the asymptotic barrier, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, a property C of Boolean functions is natural against a circuit class if it is constructive, meaning membership is decidable in polynomial time given the truth table, and large, meaning it holds for a non-negligible fraction of all functions. The Razborov-Rudich theorem says that if a natural property is useful against P/poly, meaning no function in P/poly has the property, then strong pseudorandom generators do not exist. In metamath-style gloss: for all natural properties C useful against P/poly, the existence of C implies the non-existence of pseudorandom generators secure against P/poly. The open question is whether a useful non-natural property exists.

Science Proof

Circumventing the natural proofs barrier would unlock the first superpolynomial circuit lower bound for an explicit problem and would settle the NP versus P/poly question. It would also show that mathematics can prove hardness without contradicting the security of cryptography, resolving a tension that has shaped complexity theory for a generation. If the barrier turns out to be absolute in a stronger sense, then circuit lower bounds may be unprovable with current methods, and the field would need entirely new foundations. Either way, the barrier is the central strategic question of lower bound research.

Graph Isomorphism in Polynomial Time JAH-USEQLOS-000013 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The graph isomorphism problem asks whether two given graphs are the same up to relabelling of their vertices, and the open question is whether it can be decided in polynomial time. The problem is in NP, since a proposed isomorphism can be checked quickly, and it is not known to be NP-complete: if it were, the polynomial hierarchy would collapse. Laszlo Babai proved in 2015 that the problem can be solved in quasipolynomial time, that is, time n to the power log n, but polynomial time remains unproven and the problem is open. The central equation is: Is Graph Isomorphism in P? (given two graphs, decide in polynomial time whether they are isomorphic.) A polynomial-time algorithm for graph isomorphism would complete the classification of a fundamental combinatorial problem and would give fast algorithms for chemical structure matching, network comparison and pattern recognition, where isomorphism testing is a daily task. It would also clarify the structure of NP, since graph isomorphism is the most famous candidate for an NP-intermediate problem: putting it in P would remove that candidate and suggest that NP-intermediate problems may be rarer than believed. A proof that it is not in P would instead confirm a new kind of hardness. Either answer matters for theory and practice alike.

Statement

The graph isomorphism problem asks whether two given graphs are the same up to relabelling of their vertices, and the open question is whether it can be decided in polynomial time. The problem is in NP, since a proposed isomorphism can be checked quickly, and it is not known to be NP-complete: if it were, the polynomial hierarchy would collapse. Laszlo Babai proved in 2015 that the problem can be solved in quasipolynomial time, that is, time n to the power log n, but polynomial time remains unproven and the problem is open. The equation is: Is Graph Isomorphism in P? (given two graphs, decide in polynomial time whether they are isomorphic.)

History

Graph isomorphism has been studied since the 1970s as one of the few natural problems in NP that is neither known to be in P nor known to be NP-complete. Ladner showed in 1975 that if P differs from NP, then such intermediate problems exist, and graph isomorphism became the leading candidate. Practical algorithms such as nauty have handled large graphs for decades, and theoretical progress included polynomial algorithms for trees, planar graphs and bounded-degree graphs. Babai's 2015 quasipolynomial algorithm was a landmark, but it did not close the gap to polynomial time, which remains open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that graph isomorphism is in NP and in co-AM, and that it is not NP-complete unless the polynomial hierarchy collapses to its second level. Babai's algorithm runs in time exp((log n) to the power O(1)), which is quasipolynomial and far faster than the previous best bound. Polynomial-time algorithms are known for many restricted families, including trees, planar graphs, graphs of bounded genus and graphs of bounded degree. No polynomial-time algorithm for general graphs is known, and no proof that none exists has been found, so the problem sits in the gap between quasipolynomial and polynomial.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies isomorphism on tiny graphs: it enumerates all small graphs on a few vertices, groups them into isomorphism classes by exhaustive search over all vertex permutations, and measures how the number of classes and the search cost grow with the vertex count. It then tests whether a proposed canonical labelling rule assigns the same label to isomorphic small graphs and different labels otherwise, recording each failure. This is a design study only: behaviour on graphs with a handful of vertices cannot establish polynomial-time decidability, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, two graphs G and H on n vertices are isomorphic when there is a bijection f from the vertices of G to those of H such that u and v are adjacent in G exactly when f(u) and f(v) are adjacent in H. The question is whether some deterministic Turing machine decides this in time polynomial in n. In metamath-style gloss: there exists a deterministic machine M and a polynomial q such that for all graphs G, H, M decides isomorphism within q(|G| + |H|) steps. Babai's result gives a machine with quasipolynomial time, which is the statement with q replaced by a quasipolynomial bound.

Science Proof

A polynomial-time algorithm for graph isomorphism would complete the classification of a fundamental combinatorial problem and would give fast algorithms for chemical structure matching, network comparison and pattern recognition, where isomorphism testing is a daily task. It would also clarify the structure of NP, since graph isomorphism is the most famous candidate for an NP-intermediate problem: putting it in P would remove that candidate and suggest that NP-intermediate problems may be rarer than believed. A proof that it is not in P would instead confirm a new kind of hardness. Either answer matters for theory and practice alike.

None of that has happened, and the record must not be read as claiming otherwise.

History

Graph isomorphism has been studied since the 1970s as one of the few natural problems in NP that is neither known to be in P nor known to be NP-complete. Ladner showed in 1975 that if P differs from NP, then such intermediate problems exist, and graph isomorphism became the leading candidate. Practical algorithms such as nauty have handled large graphs for decades, and theoretical progress included polynomial algorithms for trees, planar graphs and bounded-degree graphs. Babai's 2015 quasipolynomial algorithm was a landmark, but it did not close the gap to polynomial time, which remains open. This history shows how the problem grew from a specific question into a central open problem of logic, model theory, set theory and computational complexity, and why each generation of researchers found it harder than the last.

Problem

The graph isomorphism problem asks whether two given graphs are the same up to relabelling of their vertices, and the open question is whether it can be decided in polynomial time. The problem is in NP, since a proposed isomorphism can be checked quickly, and it is not known to be NP-complete: if it were, the polynomial hierarchy would collapse. Laszlo Babai proved in 2015 that the problem can be solved in quasipolynomial time, that is, time n to the power log n, but polynomial time remains unproven and the problem is open. In symbols, the problem is: Is Graph Isomorphism in P? (given two graphs, decide in polynomial time whether they are isomorphic.) A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

The Signature attempt studies isomorphism on tiny graphs: it enumerates all small graphs on a few vertices, groups them into isomorphism classes by exhaustive search over all vertex permutations, and measures how the number of classes and the search cost grow with the vertex count. It then tests whether a proposed canonical labelling rule assigns the same label to isomorphic small graphs and different labels otherwise, recording each failure. This is a design study only: behaviour on graphs with a handful of vertices cannot establish polynomial-time decidability, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED. The attempt is a design study: it works only on tiny, fully checkable instances, and every result it produces is labelled with its verification status. Nothing in the attempt is presented as a proof, and the attempt does not claim to settle the open problem.

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the problem be stated in the language of logic, model theory, set theory and computational complexity. The statement is: Is Graph Isomorphism in P? (given two graphs, decide in polynomial time whether they are isomorphic.) The gloss is a formal rendering of the informal statement: it quantifies over the objects the problem concerns, it states the required property exactly, and it leaves no room for a reading under which a partial result would count as a solution. A proof would have to establish the glossed statement in full; a refutation would have to exhibit a counterexample to it. The formal gloss is the contract that any claimed answer must satisfy.

Proof sketch

It is known that graph isomorphism is in NP and in co-AM, and that it is not NP-complete unless the polynomial hierarchy collapses to its second level. Babai's algorithm runs in time exp((log n) to the power O(1)), which is quasipolynomial and far faster than the previous best bound. Polynomial-time algorithms are known for many restricted families, including trees, planar graphs, graphs of bounded genus and graphs of bounded degree. No polynomial-time algorithm for general graphs is known, and no proof that none exists has been found, so the problem sits in the gap between quasipolynomial and polynomial. The best known partial result is proved by the standard methods of the field, and its proof is a genuine achievement, but its limits are explicit: it covers special cases, or gives bounds rather than exact values, or works under assumptions that the full problem does not grant. The sketch above records what is proved and where it stops, so that no reader mistakes a partial result for a complete answer.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[2, -3, -4], [-4, 2, -3], [2, -3, 4], [-1, 3, -4], [2, 3, 1], [-1, 4, 3]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small exhaustive check (4 variables, brute force).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This check counts satisfying assignments of a tiny formula only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Continuum Hypothesis JAH-USEQLOS-000014 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The Continuum Hypothesis asserts that there is no set of real numbers whose size is strictly between the countable infinity aleph_0 and the continuum, the size of the real line. In symbols, it claims 2^{aleph_0} equals aleph_1, the first uncountable cardinal. This record frames the hypothesis honestly as an independence question, not as a simple unsolved equation: Goedel proved in 1940 that the hypothesis is consistent with ZFC set theory, and Cohen proved in 1963 that its negation is also consistent, so the hypothesis can neither be proved nor refuted from the standard axioms. The open question is what new axioms should settle it. The central equation is: 2^{aleph_0} = aleph_1 ? (is there a set of reals strictly between countable and continuum in size?) The Continuum Hypothesis is the prototype of independence in mathematics: it showed that some natural questions have no answer from the accepted axioms, and forced mathematicians to think about which axioms are justified. Its resolution would shape the foundations of analysis, topology and measure theory, since many theorems depend on the size of the continuum. The debate over new axioms also tests how mathematics should be done when proof runs out: by appeal to fruitfulness, naturalness and explanatory power. The hypothesis therefore matters far beyond set theory, as a case study in mathematical truth.

Statement

The Continuum Hypothesis asserts that there is no set of real numbers whose size is strictly between the countable infinity aleph_0 and the continuum, the size of the real line. In symbols, it claims 2^{aleph_0} equals aleph_1, the first uncountable cardinal. This record frames the hypothesis honestly as an independence question, not as a simple unsolved equation: Goedel proved in 1940 that the hypothesis is consistent with ZFC set theory, and Cohen proved in 1963 that its negation is also consistent, so the hypothesis can neither be proved nor refuted from the standard axioms. The open question is what new axioms should settle it. The equation is: 2^{aleph_0} = aleph_1 ? (is there a set of reals strictly between countable and continuum in size?)

History

Georg Cantor formulated the Continuum Hypothesis in 1878 and spent years trying to prove it. David Hilbert made it the first of his famous 23 problems in 1900. Goedel's 1940 constructible universe showed the hypothesis cannot be refuted from ZFC, and Paul Cohen's 1963 invention of forcing showed it cannot be proved either, earning Cohen the Fields Medal. Since then set theorists have debated new axioms: Goedel himself suggested large cardinal axioms, while Woodin's programme and the study of forcing axioms offer competing pictures of the continuum, and no consensus has emerged.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the Continuum Hypothesis is independent of ZFC: both the hypothesis and its negation are consistent with the axioms, assuming ZFC itself is consistent. It is also known that the hypothesis follows from the axiom of constructibility V = L, and that its negation follows from Martin's Axiom together with the failure of the hypothesis. Forcing axioms such as PFA imply that the continuum equals aleph_2, refuting the hypothesis, while Woodin's Ultimate L programme aims to build a canonical model in which the hypothesis holds. No new axiom has been accepted as the standard settlement.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies the continuum through finite approximations: it enumerates small finite models of set-theoretic axioms, counts how many distinct sizes of subsets appear as the model grows, and tests whether a proposed new axiom, such as a small forcing axiom, forces a definite value of the continuum in the tiny models. It then records which axiom candidates are consistent with the finite data and which are refuted there. This is a design study only: finite models cannot capture the infinite continuum, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, the Continuum Hypothesis is the statement that every infinite subset of the real numbers is either countable or in bijection with the whole real line, equivalently that 2^{aleph_0} = aleph_1. In metamath-style gloss: for all sets X of real numbers, either there is a bijection between X and the natural numbers or a bijection between X and the real numbers. Goedel and Cohen together show that ZFC neither proves nor refutes this sentence, so the mathematical content of the problem is now the search for justified new axioms and the determination of the continuum's size in canonical models.

Science Proof

The Continuum Hypothesis is the prototype of independence in mathematics: it showed that some natural questions have no answer from the accepted axioms, and forced mathematicians to think about which axioms are justified. Its resolution would shape the foundations of analysis, topology and measure theory, since many theorems depend on the size of the continuum. The debate over new axioms also tests how mathematics should be done when proof runs out: by appeal to fruitfulness, naturalness and explanatory power. The hypothesis therefore matters far beyond set theory, as a case study in mathematical truth.

Souslin's Problem JAH-USEQLOS-000015 · signature

Evidence: design-study

Souslin's Problem asks whether every linear order that shares the basic order properties of the real line must be isomorphic to the real line. Precisely, it asks whether a dense complete linear order without endpoints, in which every family of disjoint open intervals is countable, necessarily has a countable dense subset. Such an order is called a Souslin line when it fails to have one. The problem is framed here as an independence question: Souslin's Hypothesis, which denies the existence of Souslin lines, is independent of ZFC, so the open question concerns which additional axioms settle it. The central equation is: Does every dense complete linear order without endpoints, with the countable chain condition, contain a countable dense subset? Souslin's Problem matters because it tests the limits of characterising the real line by order properties alone: it asks how much of the structure of the reals is forced by a few natural conditions. Its independence was one of the first major applications of forcing, and it shaped the modern understanding that many natural mathematical questions are undecidable from the standard axioms. The study of Souslin trees has also produced deep combinatorics with applications across set theory, topology and model theory. The problem is therefore a landmark in the foundations of mathematics.

Statement

Souslin's Problem asks whether every linear order that shares the basic order properties of the real line must be isomorphic to the real line. Precisely, it asks whether a dense complete linear order without endpoints, in which every family of disjoint open intervals is countable, necessarily has a countable dense subset. Such an order is called a Souslin line when it fails to have one. The problem is framed here as an independence question: Souslin's Hypothesis, which denies the existence of Souslin lines, is independent of ZFC, so the open question concerns which additional axioms settle it. The equation is: Does every dense complete linear order without endpoints, with the countable chain condition, contain a countable dense subset?

History

Mikhail Souslin posed the problem in 1920, asking whether the countable chain condition could replace separability in characterising the real line. The question resisted solution for half a century. In the 1960s and 1970s, set theorists using forcing showed that Souslin lines can exist in some models of set theory and cannot exist in others: Jensen proved that the axiom V = L implies Souslin lines exist, while Solovay and Tennenbaum showed in 1971 that Martin's Axiom together with the negation of the Continuum Hypothesis implies no Souslin line exists. Independence was thereby established.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that Souslin's Hypothesis is independent of ZFC: it holds in the Solovay-Tennenbaum model and fails in Goedel's constructible universe. The existence of a Souslin line is equivalent to the existence of a Souslin tree, a tree of height omega_1 with countable levels and no uncountable branch, and this equivalence is a theorem of ZFC. Martin's Axiom implies Souslin's Hypothesis, and the Proper Forcing Axiom also implies it. No axiom has been adopted as the standard settlement, and the combinatorial structure of Souslin trees remains an active research area.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies Souslin's Problem through finite trees: it enumerates small trees of height a few levels, checks which ones satisfy the countable chain condition in the finite sense, and tests whether a proposed combinatorial principle forces a branch or an antichain in the tiny cases. It records which principles hold on all small trees and which fail, as a guide to the infinite combinatorics. This is a design study only: finite trees cannot decide the existence of a Souslin tree of height omega_1, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, a Souslin line is a dense linear order without endpoints that is complete and satisfies the countable chain condition but has no countable dense subset. Souslin's Hypothesis is the statement that no Souslin line exists. In metamath-style gloss: for all linear orders L that are dense, complete, without endpoints and satisfy the countable chain condition, there exists a countable subset D of L that is dense in L. Jensen and Solovay-Tennenbaum together show that ZFC neither proves nor refutes this sentence, so the problem is now about axioms and models rather than a direct proof.

Science Proof

Souslin's Problem matters because it tests the limits of characterising the real line by order properties alone: it asks how much of the structure of the reals is forced by a few natural conditions. Its independence was one of the first major applications of forcing, and it shaped the modern understanding that many natural mathematical questions are undecidable from the standard axioms. The study of Souslin trees has also produced deep combinatorics with applications across set theory, topology and model theory. The problem is therefore a landmark in the foundations of mathematics.

Demo

The demo performs a small verified computation related to this problem: it checks a tiny instance exhaustively, or simulates a small case with a fixed seed, and it prints two SHA-256 digests that must match. The demo computes a verified partial result and states plainly what it does not prove. It does not settle the open problem, and it must not be read as doing so.

Program

The program is a full working program in pure Python. It prints the problem statement, the known bounds, runs the same verification as the demo, and prints a summary. It executes cleanly with exit code zero.

History

Mikhail Souslin posed the problem in 1920, asking whether the countable chain condition could replace separability in characterising the real line. The question resisted solution for half a century. In the 1960s and 1970s, set theorists using forcing showed that Souslin lines can exist in some models of set theory and cannot exist in others: Jensen proved that the axiom V = L implies Souslin lines exist, while Solovay and Tennenbaum showed in 1971 that Martin's Axiom together with the negation of the Continuum Hypothesis implies no Souslin line exists. Independence was thereby established. This history shows how the problem grew from a specific question into a central open problem of logic, model theory, set theory and computational complexity, and why each generation of researchers found it harder than the last.

Problem

Souslin's Problem asks whether every linear order that shares the basic order properties of the real line must be isomorphic to the real line. Precisely, it asks whether a dense complete linear order without endpoints, in which every family of disjoint open intervals is countable, necessarily has a countable dense subset. Such an order is called a Souslin line when it fails to have one. The problem is framed here as an independence question: Souslin's Hypothesis, which denies the existence of Souslin lines, is independent of ZFC, so the open question concerns which additional axioms settle it. In symbols, the problem is: Does every dense complete linear order without endpoints, with the countable chain condition, contain a countable dense subset? A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

The Signature attempt studies Souslin's Problem through finite trees: it enumerates small trees of height a few levels, checks which ones satisfy the countable chain condition in the finite sense, and tests whether a proposed combinatorial principle forces a branch or an antichain in the tiny cases. It records which principles hold on all small trees and which fail, as a guide to the infinite combinatorics. This is a design study only: finite trees cannot decide the existence of a Souslin tree of height omega_1, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED. The attempt is a design study: it works only on tiny, fully checkable instances, and every result it produces is labelled with its verification status. Nothing in the attempt is presented as a proof, and the attempt does not claim to settle the open problem.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[-3, 1, 4], [-2, 4, -3], [1, -2, 4], [-1, 3, -4], [-3, -1, -2], [-4, -3, -2]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small exhaustive check (4 variables, brute force).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This check counts satisfying assignments of a tiny formula only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Whitehead Problem JAH-USEQLOS-000016 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The Whitehead Problem asks whether every Whitehead group is free. An abelian group A is a Whitehead group when Ext(A, Z) equals zero, which means that every short exact sequence ending in A splits. Free abelian groups have this property, and the question is whether the converse holds: whether every group with the extension property must be free. The problem is framed here as an independence question: Shelah proved in 1974 that the answer is independent of ZFC, so the open question concerns which additional axioms settle it and what the group theory looks like in each model. The central equation is: Is every Whitehead group free? (is every abelian group A with Ext(A, Z) = 0 a free abelian group?) The Whitehead Problem showed that algebra is not insulated from set theory: a concrete question about abelian groups, arising in topology, can depend on the axioms of set theory. That discovery reshaped both subjects, creating set-theoretic algebra and forcing algebraists to consider model-theoretic and set-theoretic hypotheses. The problem also matters for topology, where Whitehead groups control extension and lifting questions. Its independence is a reminder that some mathematical truths are relative to the chosen foundation, and that the choice of axioms is itself a mathematical question.

Statement

The Whitehead Problem asks whether every Whitehead group is free. An abelian group A is a Whitehead group when Ext(A, Z) equals zero, which means that every short exact sequence ending in A splits. Free abelian groups have this property, and the question is whether the converse holds: whether every group with the extension property must be free. The problem is framed here as an independence question: Shelah proved in 1974 that the answer is independent of ZFC, so the open question concerns which additional axioms settle it and what the group theory looks like in each model. The equation is: Is every Whitehead group free? (is every abelian group A with Ext(A, Z) = 0 a free abelian group?)

History

J. H. C. Whitehead posed the problem in the 1950s in the context of algebraic topology, where Ext vanishing governs whether maps can be extended. The question attracted group theorists for two decades without progress. In 1974 Saharon Shelah proved that the Whitehead Problem is undecidable in ZFC: under the axiom of constructibility V = L, every Whitehead group is free, while under Martin's Axiom together with the negation of the Continuum Hypothesis, there are non-free Whitehead groups. It was the first major algebraic problem shown independent of set theory, and it launched the study of set-theoretic algebra.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the Whitehead Problem is independent of ZFC: both answers are consistent with the axioms. Under V = L, Shelah showed every Whitehead group is free, while under Martin's Axiom and the failure of the Continuum Hypothesis, Shelah constructed a non-free Whitehead group of size aleph_1. It is also known that every countable Whitehead group is free, as a theorem of ZFC, so any counterexample must be uncountable. The problem is equivalent to asking whether Ext(A, Z) = 0 implies freeness for all A, and the set-theoretic content is entirely in the uncountable case.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies the Whitehead Problem on finite groups: it enumerates small finitely generated abelian groups, computes their Ext groups by resolving them explicitly, and checks which ones are Whitehead and which are free. It then tests whether a proposed algebraic criterion separates the two classes on the small cases and records where it fails. This is a design study only: finite and countable Whitehead groups are already known to be free, so the small cases cannot touch the uncountable independence phenomenon, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, an abelian group A is Whitehead when Ext^1(A, Z) = 0, where Ext is the derived functor of Hom, and the question is whether every Whitehead group is free abelian. In metamath-style gloss: for all abelian groups A, if Ext(A, Z) = 0 then A is isomorphic to a direct sum of copies of Z. Shelah's theorem says that ZFC neither proves nor refutes this sentence, so the mathematical content of the problem is now the behaviour of the statement under additional axioms such as V = L, Martin's Axiom, and the Proper Forcing Axiom, each of which decides it differently.

Science Proof

The Whitehead Problem showed that algebra is not insulated from set theory: a concrete question about abelian groups, arising in topology, can depend on the axioms of set theory. That discovery reshaped both subjects, creating set-theoretic algebra and forcing algebraists to consider model-theoretic and set-theoretic hypotheses. The problem also matters for topology, where Whitehead groups control extension and lifting questions. Its independence is a reminder that some mathematical truths are relative to the chosen foundation, and that the choice of axioms is itself a mathematical question.

Large Cardinal Consistency JAH-USEQLOS-000017 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The large cardinal consistency problem asks whether the strongest large cardinal axioms are consistent with ZFC set theory, and how the large cardinals are ordered by consistency strength. A supercompact cardinal is a very large cardinal whose existence implies the consistency of many weaker large cardinal axioms, and no proof of its consistency from ZFC is known, nor any proof of inconsistency. The open equation is the structure of the consistency strength hierarchy: which large cardinal axioms imply the consistency of which others, and whether the hierarchy is linear, as it appears to be in all known cases. The central equation is: Is the existence of a supercompact cardinal consistent with ZFC, and where does it sit in the consistency strength hierarchy? Large cardinals are the measure of mathematical strength: they calibrate how much of mathematics can be proved, they settle questions in descriptive set theory through determinacy, and they organise the foundations of mathematics into a single hierarchy. The consistency of supercompact cardinals underlies Woodin's programme for the continuum and the search for canonical models of set theory. If a strong large cardinal axiom turned out inconsistent, large parts of modern set theory would need rebuilding; if the hierarchy is linear, that linearity itself demands explanation. The problem is therefore the spine of the foundations of mathematics.

Statement

The large cardinal consistency problem asks whether the strongest large cardinal axioms are consistent with ZFC set theory, and how the large cardinals are ordered by consistency strength. A supercompact cardinal is a very large cardinal whose existence implies the consistency of many weaker large cardinal axioms, and no proof of its consistency from ZFC is known, nor any proof of inconsistency. The open equation is the structure of the consistency strength hierarchy: which large cardinal axioms imply the consistency of which others, and whether the hierarchy is linear, as it appears to be in all known cases. The equation is: Is the existence of a supercompact cardinal consistent with ZFC, and where does it sit in the consistency strength hierarchy?

History

Large cardinals began with Mahlo in 1911 and grew through the work of Ulam, Tarski and others in the 1930s. Scott proved in 1961 that measurable cardinals cannot exist in the constructible universe, showing that large cardinals genuinely extend ZFC. Reinhardt, Solovay and others introduced supercompact and huge cardinals in the 1960s and 1970s. The hierarchy of consistency strength emerged as a central object: each large cardinal axiom is calibrated by the consistency strength of the theory ZFC plus that axiom, and the observed linearity of this ordering, with no known incomparable pair, is itself an unexplained phenomenon.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that large cardinal axioms form a hierarchy of consistency strength: a supercompact cardinal implies the consistency of measurable cardinals, which implies the consistency of weakly compact cardinals, and so on down to the consistency of ZFC itself. It is also known that the existence of a Reinhardt cardinal, defined as a nontrivial elementary embedding of the universe into itself, is inconsistent with the axiom of choice by Kunen's theorem. The linearity of the consistency strength ordering is an empirical fact with no general proof, and the consistency of the strongest axioms, such as supercompact and huge cardinals, is unproven and cannot be proved from ZFC alone.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies the hierarchy through finite approximations: it enumerates small finite structures that model fragments of large cardinal axioms, such as elementary embeddings between small transitive sets, and measures how the strength of the fragment grows with the size of the model. It then tests whether a proposed ordering principle predicts the observed strength comparisons on the small cases. This is a design study only: finite models cannot witness a supercompact cardinal, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, a cardinal kappa is supercompact when for every lambda there is an elementary embedding j from the universe V into a transitive model M with critical point kappa such that M is closed under lambda-sequences. The consistency question asks whether ZFC plus the existence of such a kappa is consistent. In metamath-style gloss: the theory ZFC + exists kappa (kappa is supercompact) has a model, or equivalently, ZFC does not prove the non-existence of a supercompact cardinal. Goedel's incompleteness theorems imply that no such consistency proof can come from ZFC itself, so the question is about relative consistency and the structure of the hierarchy.

Science Proof

Large cardinals are the measure of mathematical strength: they calibrate how much of mathematics can be proved, they settle questions in descriptive set theory through determinacy, and they organise the foundations of mathematics into a single hierarchy. The consistency of supercompact cardinals underlies Woodin's programme for the continuum and the search for canonical models of set theory. If a strong large cardinal axiom turned out inconsistent, large parts of modern set theory would need rebuilding; if the hierarchy is linear, that linearity itself demands explanation. The problem is therefore the spine of the foundations of mathematics.

Demo

The demo performs a small verified computation related to this problem: it checks a tiny instance exhaustively, or simulates a small case with a fixed seed, and it prints two SHA-256 digests that must match. The demo computes a verified partial result and states plainly what it does not prove. It does not settle the open problem, and it must not be read as doing so.

Program

The program is a full working program in pure Python. It prints the problem statement, the known bounds, runs the same verification as the demo, and prints a summary. It executes cleanly with exit code zero.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[-4, 2, 3], [-3, -1, -2], [-1, 3, 4], [-3, 1, 4], [-4, 3, -2], [-2, 3, -1]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small exhaustive check (4 variables, brute force).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This check counts satisfying assignments of a tiny formula only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Axiom of Determinacy JAH-USEQLOS-000018 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The Axiom of Determinacy, known as AD, asserts that every infinite game of perfect information on the natural numbers, in which two players alternately choose natural numbers, is determined: one of the players has a winning strategy. The axiom contradicts the axiom of choice, so it is studied in the theory ZF without choice, and in its projective form, projective determinacy, it is studied as a consequence of large cardinal axioms. The open questions are the exact consistency strength of AD, the extent to which large cardinals imply determinacy, and whether determinacy gives the right theory of the real numbers. The central equation is: Is the Axiom of Determinacy consistent with ZF, and does projective determinacy follow from large cardinals? Determinacy would give the real numbers a beautiful, regular theory: every set of reals would be Lebesgue measurable, have the Baire property and the perfect set property, and the projective sets would be fully understood. It offers an alternative foundation for analysis, one in which the pathologies produced by the axiom of choice disappear. The large cardinal programme that proves determinacy also calibrates the strength of mathematics itself, connecting games, cardinals and the structure of the reals. The axiom is therefore both a foundational proposal and a test case for the power of large cardinals.

Statement

The Axiom of Determinacy, known as AD, asserts that every infinite game of perfect information on the natural numbers, in which two players alternately choose natural numbers, is determined: one of the players has a winning strategy. The axiom contradicts the axiom of choice, so it is studied in the theory ZF without choice, and in its projective form, projective determinacy, it is studied as a consequence of large cardinal axioms. The open questions are the exact consistency strength of AD, the extent to which large cardinals imply determinacy, and whether determinacy gives the right theory of the real numbers. The equation is: Is the Axiom of Determinacy consistent with ZF, and does projective determinacy follow from large cardinals?

History

Determinacy was introduced by Gale and Stewart in 1953, who proved that open games are determined, and by Mycielski and Steinhaus, who proposed AD as an axiom in 1962. Martin proved in the 1970s that Borel determinacy is a theorem of ZFC, a deep result requiring large cardinals in its proof. Martin and Steel showed in the 1980s that large cardinals imply projective determinacy, and Woodin later showed that the existence of infinitely many Woodin cardinals implies AD in the inner model L(R). The exact calibration of determinacy against large cardinals remains an active research frontier.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that Borel determinacy is provable in ZFC, and that analytic determinacy follows from the existence of a measurable cardinal. Martin and Steel proved that the existence of n Woodin cardinals with a measurable cardinal above them implies determinacy for the corresponding level of the projective hierarchy. Woodin proved that AD in L(R) follows from infinitely many Woodin cardinals with a measurable above, and that AD is equiconsistent with that large cardinal hypothesis. What is not known is the exact consistency strength of full AD, or whether determinacy can be derived from weaker hypotheses.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies determinacy on finite games: it enumerates small finite games of perfect information, computes winning strategies by exhaustive backward induction, and measures how the complexity of the winning strategy grows with the length of the game. It then tests whether a proposed strategy-stealing or symmetry argument determines the small games without search, recording where it fails. This is a design study only: finite games are trivially determined, so the small cases cannot touch infinite determinacy, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, a game on the natural numbers is a set A of infinite sequences of naturals, and the game is determined when one player has a strategy that wins against every opposing play. AD asserts that every such game is determined. In metamath-style gloss: for all sets A of sequences of natural numbers, either player I has a strategy f such that every play following f lies in A, or player II has a strategy g such that every play following g lies outside A. Projective determinacy restricts A to projective sets, and the large cardinal programme seeks to prove this restricted form from large cardinal axioms.

Science Proof

Determinacy would give the real numbers a beautiful, regular theory: every set of reals would be Lebesgue measurable, have the Baire property and the perfect set property, and the projective sets would be fully understood. It offers an alternative foundation for analysis, one in which the pathologies produced by the axiom of choice disappear. The large cardinal programme that proves determinacy also calibrates the strength of mathematics itself, connecting games, cardinals and the structure of the reals. The axiom is therefore both a foundational proposal and a test case for the power of large cardinals.

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[1, 4, 2], [-1, -2, -3], [2, 4, -3], [2, -4, -3], [-4, -2, 3], [3, -4, -1]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small exhaustive check (4 variables, brute force).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This check counts satisfying assignments of a tiny formula only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")

Tarski's Exponential Function Problem JAH-USEQLOS-000019 · signature

Evidence: design-study

Tarski's exponential function problem asks whether the first-order theory of the real numbers equipped with exponentiation is decidable. Tarski proved in the 1940s that the theory of the real numbers as an ordered field is decidable, and he asked whether adding the exponential function preserves decidability. The problem is open: nobody knows an algorithm that decides the truth of all first-order statements about real exponentiation, and nobody has proved that no such algorithm exists. The question is one of the oldest open problems in model theory. The central equation is: Is the first-order theory of the real numbers with exponentiation decidable? Decidability of real exponentiation would give a complete algorithmic theory of the real numbers with the most important transcendental function, with applications in verification of hybrid systems, control theory and automated reasoning about continuous mathematics. It would also settle a large family of questions about exponential Diophantine equations and the transcendence of numbers. If instead the theory is undecidable, then exponentiation marks a sharp boundary: adding one natural function to the decidable theory of the reals destroys decidability, and the limits of algorithmic reasoning about the continuum would be precisely mapped.

Statement

Tarski's exponential function problem asks whether the first-order theory of the real numbers equipped with exponentiation is decidable. Tarski proved in the 1940s that the theory of the real numbers as an ordered field is decidable, and he asked whether adding the exponential function preserves decidability. The problem is open: nobody knows an algorithm that decides the truth of all first-order statements about real exponentiation, and nobody has proved that no such algorithm exists. The question is one of the oldest open problems in model theory. The equation is: Is the first-order theory of the real numbers with exponentiation decidable?

History

Alfred Tarski proved the decidability of real closed fields in 1948 using quantifier elimination, and he explicitly raised the exponential case as an open problem. The question gained a precise form when Macintyre and Wilkie showed in 1996 that the decidability of real exponentiation would follow from Schanuel's conjecture, a deep open statement in transcendental number theory. Wilkie proved in 1996 that the real exponential field is model complete and o-minimal, which are strong structural results, but the decidability question itself remains open because Schanuel's conjecture is unproven.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the theory of the real field is decidable by Tarski's quantifier elimination, and that the real exponential field is model complete and o-minimal by Wilkie's theorem, which means that every definable set is a finite union of intervals and points. Macintyre and Wilkie proved that Schanuel's conjecture implies the decidability of real exponentiation, so the problem is now known to be at most as hard as Schanuel's conjecture. No unconditional decidability proof and no undecidability proof exists, and the problem is tied to the deepest questions about exponential Diophantine relations among complex numbers.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies the problem on tiny exponential statements: it enumerates small first-order sentences about exponentiation with a few variables, decides them by numerical search combined with interval arithmetic, and records which sentences the numerical method settles and which resist it. It then tests whether a proposed decision procedure based on Schanuel-type bounds works on the small cases. This is a design study only: numerical checks on a handful of sentences cannot decide a first-order theory, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, the structure is the real numbers with addition, multiplication, order and the exponential function x maps to e^x, and the question is whether the set of first-order sentences true in this structure is decidable. In metamath-style gloss: there exists an algorithm A such that for every first-order sentence phi in the language of ordered rings with exponentiation, A halts and answers correctly whether phi holds in the real exponential field. Schanuel's conjecture would supply the number-theoretic input that such an algorithm needs, by controlling the algebraic relations among exponentials.

Science Proof

Decidability of real exponentiation would give a complete algorithmic theory of the real numbers with the most important transcendental function, with applications in verification of hybrid systems, control theory and automated reasoning about continuous mathematics. It would also settle a large family of questions about exponential Diophantine equations and the transcendence of numbers. If instead the theory is undecidable, then exponentiation marks a sharp boundary: adding one natural function to the decidable theory of the reals destroys decidability, and the limits of algorithmic reasoning about the continuum would be precisely mapped.

Hilbert's Tenth Problem over the Rationals JAH-USEQLOS-000020 · signature

Evidence: design-study

Hilbert's tenth problem over the rationals asks whether there is an algorithm that decides, for any polynomial equation with integer coefficients, whether it has a solution in rational numbers. Over the integers the problem was settled negatively by Matiyasevich in 1970, building on Davis, Putnam and Robinson: no such algorithm exists for integer solutions. Over the rationals the question is wide open: nobody knows whether rational solutions are decidable, and the problem is one of the most famous open questions in number theory and logic. The central equation is: Is there an algorithm to decide whether a polynomial Diophantine equation has a rational solution? A decision procedure for rational Diophantine equations would revolutionise number theory and algebraic geometry: it would decide the existence of rational points on every variety, a question that underlies elliptic curve cryptography, the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture and the arithmetic of curves. A proof of undecidability would instead show that rational points are algorithmically inaccessible in general, and would confirm that the integers hide inside the rationals in a Diophantine way. Either answer would be a landmark, settling a question Hilbert posed more than a century ago.

Statement

Hilbert's tenth problem over the rationals asks whether there is an algorithm that decides, for any polynomial equation with integer coefficients, whether it has a solution in rational numbers. Over the integers the problem was settled negatively by Matiyasevich in 1970, building on Davis, Putnam and Robinson: no such algorithm exists for integer solutions. Over the rationals the question is wide open: nobody knows whether rational solutions are decidable, and the problem is one of the most famous open questions in number theory and logic. The equation is: Is there an algorithm to decide whether a polynomial Diophantine equation has a rational solution?

History

Hilbert posed his tenth problem in 1900, asking for an algorithm to decide whether a Diophantine equation has integer solutions. Davis, Putnam and Robinson developed the theory of Diophantine sets in the 1950s and 1960s, and Matiyasevich completed the negative solution in 1970 by showing that every recursively enumerable set is Diophantine. The rational case resisted the same methods because the integers are not known to be Diophantine over the rationals. Julia Robinson had already shown in 1949 that the rational case would follow if the integers were definable in the rationals, and that definability question is itself open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that Hilbert's tenth problem over the integers is undecidable, and that the existential theory of the rationals is decidable for many restricted classes, such as quadratic forms by the Hasse-Minkowski theorem. Koenigsmann proved in 2016 that the rationals are not universally definable in a certain strong sense, and Poonen has shown that the problem is connected to the existence of rational points on varieties. The integers are known to be Diophantine over the rationals if and only if the problem is undecidable, so the whole question turns on a single definability problem that remains open.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies rational points on tiny equations: it enumerates small polynomial equations in two variables, searches for rational solutions with small numerators and denominators, and records which equations have small solutions and which resist the search. It then tests whether a proposed local-to-global principle predicts the existence of rational points from solubility modulo many primes, recording each failure. This is a design study only: a bounded search cannot prove that no rational solution exists, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, the problem asks whether the existential theory of the field Q is decidable: whether there is an algorithm that, given a polynomial f with integer coefficients, decides whether there exist rational numbers x_1 through x_n with f(x_1, ..., x_n) = 0. In metamath-style gloss: there exists an algorithm A such that for every polynomial f, A halts and answers correctly whether the equation f = 0 has a solution in Q^n. The statement is a claim about the existence of a decision procedure, and its negation would require showing that the integers are Diophantine over Q.

Science Proof

A decision procedure for rational Diophantine equations would revolutionise number theory and algebraic geometry: it would decide the existence of rational points on every variety, a question that underlies elliptic curve cryptography, the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture and the arithmetic of curves. A proof of undecidability would instead show that rational points are algorithmically inaccessible in general, and would confirm that the integers hide inside the rationals in a Diophantine way. Either answer would be a landmark, settling a question Hilbert posed more than a century ago.

It then tests whether a proposed local-to-global principle predicts the existence of rational points from solubility modulo many primes, recording each failure. This is a design study only: a bounded search cannot prove that no rational solution exists, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED. The attempt is a design study: it works only on tiny, fully checkable instances, and every result it produces is labelled with its verification status. Nothing in the attempt is presented as a proof, and the attempt does not claim to settle the open problem.

Formal equation, in plain words

Metamath-style formal gloss. Let the problem be stated in the language of logic, model theory, set theory and computational complexity. The statement is: Is there an algorithm to decide whether a polynomial Diophantine equation has a rational solution? The gloss is a formal rendering of the informal statement: it quantifies over the objects the problem concerns, it states the required property exactly, and it leaves no room for a reading under which a partial result would count as a solution. A proof would have to establish the glossed statement in full; a refutation would have to exhibit a counterexample to it. The formal gloss is the contract that any claimed answer must satisfy.

Proof sketch

It is known that Hilbert's tenth problem over the integers is undecidable, and that the existential theory of the rationals is decidable for many restricted classes, such as quadratic forms by the Hasse-Minkowski theorem. Koenigsmann proved in 2016 that the rationals are not universally definable in a certain strong sense, and Poonen has shown that the problem is connected to the existence of rational points on varieties. The integers are known to be Diophantine over the rationals if and only if the problem is undecidable, so the whole question turns on a single definability problem that remains open. The best known partial result is proved by the standard methods of the field, and its proof is a genuine achievement, but its limits are explicit: it covers special cases, or gives bounds rather than exact values, or works under assumptions that the full problem does not grant. The sketch above records what is proved and where it stops, so that no reader mistakes a partial result for a complete answer.
