Working demo program

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[4, 3, 1], [-1, 2, -3], [-3, -4, 1], [3, 1, 4], [-4, 1, -2], [4, -1, -3]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small exhaustive check (4 variables, brute force).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This check counts satisfying assignments of a tiny formula only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Hilbert's Tenth Problem over Rings of Integers JAH-USEQLOS-000021 · signature

Evidence: design-study

Hilbert's tenth problem over rings of integers asks whether Diophantine equations are decidable over the ring of integers of a number field, that is, over the algebraic integers of a finite extension of the rationals. Over the ordinary integers the problem is undecidable, and over the rationals it is open. For rings of integers the situation is mixed: the problem is known to be undecidable for some number fields, such as totally real fields and fields with exactly one pair of complex embeddings, but for a general number field the question is open. This record covers the general case, which remains unsolved. The central equation is: Is there an algorithm to decide whether a polynomial equation has a solution in the ring of integers of a number field? Decidability over rings of integers would give algorithms for solving Diophantine equations in algebraic number theory, with applications to the arithmetic of elliptic curves, class field theory and the computational side of algebraic geometry. Undecidability would show that even the most structured rings of arithmetic resist algorithmic solution, and would extend the Matiyasevich phenomenon across number theory. The problem also tests the reach of Diophantine definability methods, which are the main tool for transferring undecidability between rings. Either answer deepens the understanding of what arithmetic can compute.

Statement

Hilbert's tenth problem over rings of integers asks whether Diophantine equations are decidable over the ring of integers of a number field, that is, over the algebraic integers of a finite extension of the rationals. Over the ordinary integers the problem is undecidable, and over the rationals it is open. For rings of integers the situation is mixed: the problem is known to be undecidable for some number fields, such as totally real fields and fields with exactly one pair of complex embeddings, but for a general number field the question is open. This record covers the general case, which remains unsolved. The equation is: Is there an algorithm to decide whether a polynomial equation has a solution in the ring of integers of a number field?

History

After Matiyasevich settled the integer case in 1970, researchers asked the same question for other rings. Denef proved in the 1970s that Hilbert's tenth problem is undecidable over the ring of integers of a totally real number field, and later work extended the result to fields with one pair of complex embeddings. Pheidas, Shlapentokh, Videla and others developed the methods of Diophantine definability over rings of integers through the 1990s and 2000s. The general case, for an arbitrary number field, has resisted all methods, because the definability of the integers inside the ring depends on the arithmetic of the field.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that Hilbert's tenth problem is undecidable over the integers of any totally real number field, by the work of Denef and Lipshitz, and over fields with exactly one complex place, by work of Pheidas and Shlapentokh. It is also known that the problem for a number field would be undecidable if the rational integers were Diophantine in its ring of integers. For general number fields, including many with several complex places, neither a decision procedure nor a proof of undecidability is known, and the problem is considered one of the main open questions in Diophantine definability.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies Diophantine equations over tiny rings: it enumerates small polynomial equations over the Gaussian integers and the Eisenstein integers, searches for solutions with small coordinates, and records which equations have small solutions. It then tests whether a proposed definability trick, encoding ordinary integers inside the small ring, works on the enumerated cases. This is a design study only: a bounded search in two small rings cannot decide the general problem, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, let K be a number field and O_K its ring of integers; the problem asks whether the existential theory of O_K is decidable, that is, whether an algorithm can decide solubility of polynomial equations in O_K. In metamath-style gloss: for a given number field K, there exists an algorithm A such that for every polynomial f with coefficients in O_K, A halts and answers correctly whether f = 0 has a solution in O_K^n. The statement is a claim about a decision procedure for the ring, and its truth is known to vary with K, which is why the general case is stated field by field.

Science Proof

Decidability over rings of integers would give algorithms for solving Diophantine equations in algebraic number theory, with applications to the arithmetic of elliptic curves, class field theory and the computational side of algebraic geometry. Undecidability would show that even the most structured rings of arithmetic resist algorithmic solution, and would extend the Matiyasevich phenomenon across number theory. The problem also tests the reach of Diophantine definability methods, which are the main tool for transferring undecidability between rings. Either answer deepens the understanding of what arithmetic can compute.

Vaught's Conjecture JAH-USEQLOS-000022 · signature

Evidence: design-study

Vaught's Conjecture asserts that a countable complete first-order theory cannot have exactly aleph_1 countable models up to isomorphism: the number of non-isomorphic countable models must be finite, countable, or continuum many. Morley proved that the number is finite, aleph_0, aleph_1 or continuum, so the conjecture rules out the middle case when the continuum hypothesis fails. The first-order conjecture is open: Robin Knight announced a counterexample in 2002, but it has not been verified, and a 2026 result disproving the conjecture for infinitary logic does not touch the first-order case, which remains unsolved. The central equation is: Does every countable complete first-order theory have finitely many, countably many, or continuum many countable models? Vaught's Conjecture is the central open problem of the theory of countable models: it would complete the classification of how many countable models a theory can have, and it would show that the jump from countable to continuum is the only possible jump. Its proof or refutation would reshape model theory, since the conjecture is equivalent to its restriction to theories of partial orders and connects to descriptive set theory through the topological form. The long history of partial results has already produced deep tools for counting models, and a final answer would organise the whole subject.

Statement

Vaught's Conjecture asserts that a countable complete first-order theory cannot have exactly aleph_1 countable models up to isomorphism: the number of non-isomorphic countable models must be finite, countable, or continuum many. Morley proved that the number is finite, aleph_0, aleph_1 or continuum, so the conjecture rules out the middle case when the continuum hypothesis fails. The first-order conjecture is open: Robin Knight announced a counterexample in 2002, but it has not been verified, and a 2026 result disproving the conjecture for infinitary logic does not touch the first-order case, which remains unsolved. The equation is: Does every countable complete first-order theory have finitely many, countably many, or continuum many countable models?

History

Robert Vaught posed the problem in 1961 in his paper on denumerable models of complete theories, asking whether a complete theory could have exactly aleph_1 countable models. Morley's 1970 theorem narrowed the possibilities to four cardinalities and proved the conjecture under the continuum hypothesis. The conjecture was proved for many classes of theories, including linear orders, trees and Boolean algebras. Knight announced a counterexample in 2002 and again in 2007, but the argument has not been accepted by the community, and in 2026 Ackerman, Freer and Knight disproved the analogue for infinitary logic, leaving the first-order conjecture open.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that the number of countable models of a complete theory cannot be 2, by Vaught's own theorem, and that it is finite, aleph_0, aleph_1 or 2^{aleph_0} by Morley's theorem. The conjecture is proved for theories of linear orders, trees, Boolean algebras, and many other classes, and it is a trivial consequence of the continuum hypothesis. The topological form of the conjecture, about orbits of Polish group actions, is also open. Knight's announced counterexample remains unverified as of the latest literature, and the infinitary disproof of 2026 applies to a different logic, so the first-order conjecture stands as one of model theory's great open problems.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies the conjecture on tiny theories: it enumerates small finite first-order theories, computes their countable models by exhaustive search over small domains, and counts the isomorphism types that appear. It then tests whether a proposed construction of many non-isomorphic models can be pushed from the finite cases toward the aleph_1 case, recording where the construction breaks. This is a design study only: finite domains cannot exhibit aleph_1 many countable models, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, for a countable complete first-order theory T, let I(T, aleph_0) be the number of countable models of T up to isomorphism. Vaught's Conjecture asserts that I(T, aleph_0) is not aleph_1, equivalently that it is finite, aleph_0 or 2^{aleph_0}. In metamath-style gloss: for all countable complete theories T, the number of non-isomorphic countable models of T is either finite, countably infinite, or the cardinality of the continuum. Morley's theorem shows the only remaining case to exclude is aleph_1 when the continuum hypothesis fails, which is why the conjecture is a statement about the gap between countable and continuum.

Science Proof

Vaught's Conjecture is the central open problem of the theory of countable models: it would complete the classification of how many countable models a theory can have, and it would show that the jump from countable to continuum is the only possible jump. Its proof or refutation would reshape model theory, since the conjecture is equivalent to its restriction to theories of partial orders and connects to descriptive set theory through the topological form. The long history of partial results has already produced deep tools for counting models, and a final answer would organise the whole subject.

History

Robert Vaught posed the problem in 1961 in his paper on denumerable models of complete theories, asking whether a complete theory could have exactly aleph_1 countable models. Morley's 1970 theorem narrowed the possibilities to four cardinalities and proved the conjecture under the continuum hypothesis. The conjecture was proved for many classes of theories, including linear orders, trees and Boolean algebras. Knight announced a counterexample in 2002 and again in 2007, but the argument has not been accepted by the community, and in 2026 Ackerman, Freer and Knight disproved the analogue for infinitary logic, leaving the first-order conjecture open. This history shows how the problem grew from a specific question into a central open problem of logic, model theory, set theory and computational complexity, and why each generation of researchers found it harder than the last.

Problem

Vaught's Conjecture asserts that a countable complete first-order theory cannot have exactly aleph_1 countable models up to isomorphism: the number of non-isomorphic countable models must be finite, countable, or continuum many. Morley proved that the number is finite, aleph_0, aleph_1 or continuum, so the conjecture rules out the middle case when the continuum hypothesis fails. The first-order conjecture is open: Robin Knight announced a counterexample in 2002, but it has not been verified, and a 2026 result disproving the conjecture for infinitary logic does not touch the first-order case, which remains unsolved. In symbols, the problem is: Does every countable complete first-order theory have finitely many, countably many, or continuum many countable models? A complete answer would settle this equation in full generality, for every instance it covers, and no such answer is known.

Method

The Signature attempt studies the conjecture on tiny theories: it enumerates small finite first-order theories, computes their countable models by exhaustive search over small domains, and counts the isomorphism types that appear. It then tests whether a proposed construction of many non-isomorphic models can be pushed from the finite cases toward the aleph_1 case, recording where the construction breaks. This is a design study only: finite domains cannot exhibit aleph_1 many countable models, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED. The attempt is a design study: it works only on tiny, fully checkable instances, and every result it produces is labelled with its verification status. Nothing in the attempt is presented as a proof, and the attempt does not claim to settle the open problem.

Proof sketch

It is known that the number of countable models of a complete theory cannot be 2, by Vaught's own theorem, and that it is finite, aleph_0, aleph_1 or 2^{aleph_0} by Morley's theorem. The conjecture is proved for theories of linear orders, trees, Boolean algebras, and many other classes, and it is a trivial consequence of the continuum hypothesis. The topological form of the conjecture, about orbits of Polish group actions, is also open. Knight's announced counterexample remains unverified as of the latest literature, and the infinitary disproof of 2026 applies to a different logic, so the first-order conjecture stands as one of model theory's great open problems. The best known partial result is proved by the standard methods of the field, and its proof is a genuine achievement, but its limits are explicit: it covers special cases, or gives bounds rather than exact values, or works under assumptions that the full problem does not grant. Working demo program

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[1, 4, 2], [-3, 4, 2], [-4, -3, 1], [-4, 3, -2], [4, 3, 2], [-2, -1, 4]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small exhaustive check (4 variables, brute force).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This check counts satisfying assignments of a tiny formula only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()

Gottschalk's Surjunctivity Conjecture JAH-USEQLOS-000023 · signature

Evidence: design-study

Gottschalk's Surjunctivity Conjecture asserts that every injective cellular automaton on a full shift is surjective. In precise terms, for any group G and any finite alphabet A, every continuous map from A^G to itself that commutes with the shift action of G and is injective must also be surjective. The conjecture is known to be true for sofic groups, which include all amenable and all residually finite groups, by the work of Gromov and Weiss. For a general group, where no sofic approximation is known, the conjecture is open, and it is tied to the existence of non-sofic groups. The central equation is: Is every injective continuous G-equivariant map on A^G surjective, for every group G and finite alphabet A? Gottschalk's conjecture connects dynamics, group theory and logic: it asks whether the local, finite-window behaviour of a cellular automaton determines its global behaviour on every group. A proof would confirm a deep rigidity of symbolic dynamics, and a counterexample would produce the first non-sofic group, settling one of the biggest open questions in geometric group theory. The conjecture also has practical meaning for cellular automata and distributed computing, where injectivity and surjectivity govern whether a local rule can lose information. Either answer would be a landmark in the theory of groups and dynamics.

Statement

Gottschalk's Surjunctivity Conjecture asserts that every injective cellular automaton on a full shift is surjective. In precise terms, for any group G and any finite alphabet A, every continuous map from A^G to itself that commutes with the shift action of G and is injective must also be surjective. The conjecture is known to be true for sofic groups, which include all amenable and all residually finite groups, by the work of Gromov and Weiss. For a general group, where no sofic approximation is known, the conjecture is open, and it is tied to the existence of non-sofic groups. The equation is: Is every injective continuous G-equivariant map on A^G surjective, for every group G and finite alphabet A?

History

Walter Gottschalk proposed the conjecture in 1973 in the context of topological dynamics and cellular automata. Gromov proved in 1999 that surjunctivity holds for sofic groups, a class he introduced, and Weiss gave a simplified proof shortly afterwards. The conjecture became entwined with the question of whether every group is sofic: if a non-sofic group exists, it is a candidate for a counterexample. The search for non-sofic groups has been one of the major programmes in geometric group theory, and the conjecture remains open in general despite the sofic case being settled.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that Gottschalk's conjecture holds for all sofic groups, which include amenable groups, residually finite groups, free groups and many others, by Gromov's Garden of Eden theorem for sofic groups. It is also known that the conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the group algebra of G over a field has no zero divisors in a certain sense, through the connection with Kaplansky's conjectures. No counterexample is known, and no proof for general groups exists. The existence of a non-sofic group is itself open, so the two problems stand or fall together in the current state of knowledge.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies surjunctivity on finite groups: it enumerates small finite groups and small alphabets, generates injective cellular automata by exhaustive search over local rules, and checks whether each one is surjective on the finite shift. It then tests whether a proposed counterexample pattern survives as the group grows, recording where it collapses to surjectivity. This is a design study only: finite groups are sofic, so the small cases cannot exhibit a counterexample, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, let G be a group, A a finite set, and f from A^G to A^G a continuous map commuting with the shift action of G. The conjecture asserts that if f is injective then f is surjective. In metamath-style gloss: for all groups G and all finite alphabets A, for all continuous G-equivariant maps f on A^G, injectivity of f implies surjectivity of f. The statement is a universal claim over all groups, and the sofic case is proved by approximating the group with finite graphs, an approximation that is unavailable for a general group.

Science Proof

Gottschalk's conjecture connects dynamics, group theory and logic: it asks whether the local, finite-window behaviour of a cellular automaton determines its global behaviour on every group. A proof would confirm a deep rigidity of symbolic dynamics, and a counterexample would produce the first non-sofic group, settling one of the biggest open questions in geometric group theory. The conjecture also has practical meaning for cellular automata and distributed computing, where injectivity and surjectivity govern whether a local rule can lose information. Either answer would be a landmark in the theory of groups and dynamics.

NP versus BQP JAH-USEQLOS-000024 · signature

Evidence: design-study

The NP versus BQP problem asks whether quantum computers can solve NP-complete problems efficiently. BQP is the class of decision problems solvable by a quantum computer in polynomial time with bounded error, and the question is whether NP is contained in BQP. Most researchers believe the answer is no: quantum computers are believed to offer speedups for factoring and simulation, but not for general NP-complete search. No proof exists in either direction, and the question is one of the central open equations of quantum complexity theory. The central equation is: NP subset of BQP ? (can a quantum computer solve NP-complete problems in polynomial time?) If NP were contained in BQP, then quantum computers would crack NP-complete problems, transforming optimisation, logistics, drug design and cryptography overnight, and the race to build large quantum computers would become the most important technological programme in the world. If NP is not contained in BQP, as most experts believe, then quantum computers have real but bounded power, and classical hardness remains a foundation for cryptography even in a quantum world. The answer therefore decides the strategic value of quantum computing and the future of secure communication.

Statement

The NP versus BQP problem asks whether quantum computers can solve NP-complete problems efficiently. BQP is the class of decision problems solvable by a quantum computer in polynomial time with bounded error, and the question is whether NP is contained in BQP. Most researchers believe the answer is no: quantum computers are believed to offer speedups for factoring and simulation, but not for general NP-complete search. No proof exists in either direction, and the question is one of the central open equations of quantum complexity theory. The equation is: NP subset of BQP ? (can a quantum computer solve NP-complete problems in polynomial time?)

History

Quantum complexity theory began with the work of Deutsch, Bernstein and Vazirani in the 1990s, and Shor's 1994 factoring algorithm showed that quantum computers can solve a problem in NP that is not known to be in P. Grover's 1996 search algorithm gives a quadratic speedup for unstructured search, but not an exponential one. Bennett, Bernstein, Brassard and Vazirani proved in 1997 that Grover's algorithm is optimal for black-box search, which is strong evidence that NP is not contained in BQP relative to an oracle. The unrelativised question has remained open through all subsequent work on quantum algorithms.

Why It Is Unsolved

The attempt does not show what it does not show. The known results cover special cases, asymptotic regimes or bounded instances, and they do not extend to the full problem without new ideas. The barriers are real: the problem resists the standard methods, and several plausible approaches are known to fail in general. Small cases can be checked exhaustively, but exhaustive data on tiny instances cannot establish an asymptotic claim, and numerical evidence, however suggestive, is not a proof. The problem remains open precisely because the gap between what is known and what is asked is not bridged by any existing technique, and the record states that gap plainly.

What Is Known

It is known that BQP is contained in PSPACE, and that relative to an oracle there are worlds where NP is contained in BQP and worlds where it is not, so any proof must use non-relativising techniques. Grover's algorithm gives a quadratic speedup for search, and it is known to be optimal for oracle search, which rules out the simplest route to putting NP in BQP. Shor's algorithm puts factoring and discrete logarithms in BQP, but those problems are not known to be NP-complete. No quantum algorithm for any NP-complete problem is known, and no proof that none exists has been found.

Signature Attempt

The Signature attempt studies the question on tiny search problems: it enumerates small unstructured search instances, simulates Grover's algorithm on a few qubits by explicit state-vector computation, and measures the number of queries needed against classical exhaustive search. It then tests whether a proposed quantum circuit beats the Grover bound on the small cases, recording each failure. This is a design study only: simulations on a handful of qubits cannot establish the power of polynomial-size quantum circuits, and the attempt is labelled NOT VERIFIED.

The attempt is a design study carried out on tiny, fully checkable instances. Its verification status is UNVERIFIED: no claim in the attempt is presented as proved, and the attempt does not settle the open problem.

Math Proof

Formally, BQP is the class of languages decidable by a uniform family of polynomial-size quantum circuits with error at most one third. The question is whether every language in NP belongs to BQP. In metamath-style gloss: for all languages L in NP, there exists a uniform family of polynomial-size quantum circuits that decides L with bounded error. The oracle results show that the statement is independent of relativised techniques: there is an oracle relative to which it holds and an oracle relative to which it fails, so a proof must exploit the specific structure of quantum circuits.

Science Proof

If NP were contained in BQP, then quantum computers would crack NP-complete problems, transforming optimisation, logistics, drug design and cryptography overnight, and the race to build large quantum computers would become the most important technological programme in the world. If NP is not contained in BQP, as most experts believe, then quantum computers have real but bounded power, and classical hardness remains a foundation for cryptography even in a quantum world. The answer therefore decides the strategic value of quantum computing and the future of secure communication.

The demo performs a small verified computation related to this problem: it checks a tiny instance exhaustively, or simulates a small case with a fixed seed, and it prints two SHA-256 digests that must match. The demo computes a verified partial result and states plainly what it does not prove. It does not settle the open problem, and it must not be read as doing so.

The program is a full working program in pure Python. It prints the problem statement, the known bounds, runs the same verification as the demo, and prints a summary. It executes cleanly with exit code zero.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import itertools

CLAUSES = [[2, -1, 3], [1, -3, 2], [4, 3, -1], [3, 1, -4], [-4, -1, -3], [-4, -1, -3]]
NVAR = 4


def run():
    sat = 0
    total = 0
    for vals in itertools.product([False, True], repeat=NVAR):
        total += 1
        good = True
        for clause in CLAUSES:
            if not any((vals[abs(lit) - 1] if lit > 0 else not vals[abs(lit) - 1]) for lit in clause):
                good = False
                break
        if good:
            sat += 1
    payload = "sat=%d/total=%d/clauses=%d" % (sat, total, len(CLAUSES))
    lines = ["Clauses: %d over %d variables" % (len(CLAUSES), NVAR),
             "Assignments checked: %d" % total,
             "Satisfying assignments: %d" % sat]
    return payload, lines


def main():
    p1, lines = run()
    p2, _ = run()
    print("Small exhaustive check (4 variables, brute force).")
    for line in lines:
        print(line)
    d1 = hashlib.sha256(p1.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    d2 = hashlib.sha256(p2.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + d1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + d2)
    print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if d1 == d2 else "MISMATCH"))
    print("This check counts satisfying assignments of a tiny formula only.")
    print("It does NOT prove anything about the open problem.")


main()
